# Findings — Bugs Bunny: Lost in Time, from 256 on

This list **continues the numbering of Ombelll's findings**
([Bugs-bunny-lost-in-time-reverse-engineered](https://github.com/Ombelll/Bugs-bunny-lost-in-time-reverse-engineered),
findings 1 to 255) and keeps their form: a number, the finding, and **the test that could have
failed**. Findings 256 to 374 were made while building the
[BBLIT Viewer](https://github.com/AleMastroianni/BBLIT-Viewer) and while reading the PC executable;
the notes at the end ([Notes from the executable](#notes-from-the-executable)) are what was read in
the PC and PlayStation executables and never became a numbered finding.

How to read a row, what the statuses `CODE`, `GAME`, `DATA` and `HYPOTHESIS` mean, which versions
the addresses hold for (`bugs.exe` 1.0 on the PC, the USA disc on the PlayStation) and an index by
topic are in the [README](README.md). What was believed and turned out wrong, and the blind checks
that missed, are in [REJECTED_HYPOTHESES.md](REJECTED_HYPOTHESES.md); a finding that was corrected
later keeps its row here and **ends with a pointer to what corrects it**.

In the rows: a bare number is a finding (1 to 255 are Ombelll's; a number written with an O in front, as O102, is one of theirs);
"level byte" and "save byte" are the two tables of 256
bytes the rules of the game read and write (see 331 and 335). The names given to functions and fields (`level_load`, `spawn_from_template`, `object_delete`…) are labels given here or in Ombelll's documents, not symbols of the executable. In the test column, the *bar* is the pass mark set before a count, the *null* is what the same count gives by chance, and *without power* means passed on too few cases, or against a null too close to the bar, to mean much. Counts over the whole disc were made from lists
generated from the level files, following the readings given here; the lists are not published. Where a finding cites a recording, it is Bugs's position read tick by tick while the game runs: with Cheat Engine on the PC version, or with the BizHawk emulator on the PlayStation version. The recordings are not published. A row too long for one line of the table continues in the row "N, part 2" right under it: the two are one finding. An N-number in brackets names the working note a row was written from. Only the ten notes that never became a finding are published, at the end of this file; the content of the others is inside the findings they led to (the table at the head of that section says which).

---

## Findings 256 to 374

| # | Finding | Test that could have failed |
|---|---|---|
| 256 | **A terrain sector header always takes up at least one bounding face, even when it declares none:** the point block starts at `8 + max(n_bound_faces, 1) * 8`, not at `+8`. `DATA`. A terrain sector with `mode 0x0000` declares `n_bound_faces = 0`, and a parser that trusts the count looks for the point block right after the 8-byte header. It is not there: one 8-byte bounding face slot is always present, used or not. This agrees with the dispatcher step Ombelll's model-format document took from the function at `0x423EB0` (in the other build: the one Ombelll's documents describe, see 264), which skips one face anyway, and with its table of "single" modes (16, 16, 56, 56, 20 bytes). For a tool: read the header, skip `max(n_bound_faces, 1)` slots of 8 bytes, then read the points; the sum of the `+0` fields of all the sectors of a level must equal the level's `n_prim`, and the last sector must end exactly on `vert_top`: both are cheap checks that catch a wrong offset at once. Measured on *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) only; sectors that have no header at all are a separate case (see 289). | The sector chain of `L03A` had to close. With this reading it does, exactly: 158 sectors, sum of the `+0` fields **3058 = `n_prim`**, every polygon chain ending on the last byte of its own record, the last sector ending exactly on `vert_top`. With the point block at `+8` the parser stopped after **3 sectors of 158**. *Held.* |
| 257 | **A quad is Z-shaped, not a fan: its two triangles are `(0,1,2)` and `(1,3,2)`.** `DATA`. The four vertices of a quad are stored in Z order (two rows), so the shared diagonal is v1–v2. An exporter that writes `f v0 v1 v2 v3` into an OBJ and lets the importer close the polygon gets `(0,1,2)` and `(0,2,3)`: the other diagonal, and on a non-planar quad a different surface. The format itself is not in question (Ombelll's documents say it in one line about exporting), but it is easy to lose in a tool: write triangles, never four-sided polygons. In *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) 87% of the terrain faces are quads (2,378 of the 2,742 faces read; the 3,058 of 256 are primitive records), so the effect is everywhere: with the wrong diagonal the piers showed light and dark sawteeth along their whole length, because the vertex colours are interpolated across the wrong edge. The same diagonal holds on the PC by the code: quads are handed to OpenGL as `GL_QUADS` in the order v2, v3, v1, v0 of the Z order, which the driver splits along v1–v2, as on the PlayStation (see 307; the split itself is the driver's, so that last step is `HYPOTHESIS`). **Read in the code by 307.** | Counting, on `L03A`, the quads whose area changes between the two readings: if the quads were flat or the order were a fan, none or almost none would. **516 of 2378 (22%) change area**, and the sawteeth along the piers disappear with the Z reading. *Held.* |
| 258 | **Face modes `0x4A` and `0x4E` are textured:** the field at `+4` names a real texture, which is a 4×4 image of one solid colour; the record has no UV because none are needed. `DATA`. Ombelll's model-format document says of these modes "texture index at +4, no UV in the record", and from there it is easy to conclude that they are flat-colour faces to be drawn with the vertex colours alone. They are not. The vertex colours of those faces are almost neutral, so drawn without the texture they come out white or grey, and in *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) they are almost half the faces of the level (the whole sea, for one: see 268). For a tool: treat `0x4A` (28-byte records) and `0x4E` (32-byte records) as textured faces that span the whole texture; texture id at `+4`, colours from `+8`. The code of the PC agrees: no UV, the whole texture, colours at `+8`, texture at `+4`, blend byte at `+6` (see 307; on how the PC maps "the whole texture", 0.01 … 0.99, see 341). A warning kept on purpose: the test "the id at `+4` is a slot the level registers" gives 100%, but a random number among the 456 ids of the table gives **97%**, because the level registers 442 of them: that number looked like a confirmation and said nothing. | Looking at the textures named: if `+4` were not a texture id, the images found there would be arbitrary. Of the 45 textures referenced by the UV-less modes in `L03A`, **45 of 45 are 4×4 and perfectly uniform**, one repeated colour, so that sampling them anywhere gives the same result. *Held.* The "registered id" test was run too and is reported as having **no power** (100% against a null of 97%). |
| 259 | **`FF FF` in the first UV pair of a face is the corner (255, 255) of the texture, not padding;** a face is untextured when its texture id is not in the slot table. `DATA`. Corrects a statement of Ombelll's model-format document: "For untextured primitives, those spots hold `FF FF` as padding". A reader that follows it discards every face whose first UV pair is `FF FF`, and in *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) that is **1158 of the 3048 faces in UV modes: 38% of the textured faces of the level** disappear. They are ordinary textured faces whose first corner happens to be the bottom right texel: the pairs read are regular corners, for example `(255,255), (255,0), (0,255), (0,0)`. For a tool: never decide "untextured" from the UV bytes. The criterion that works is the texture id: a face has no texture when its id does not exist in the table of registered slots (and see 275 for the ids that no file registers and that are nevertheless filled, at run time, by animated textures). | Three counts on the 1158 faces of `L03A` with `FF FF` first, each of which could have shown padding: the texture id is a registered slot in **1158 of 1158**; faces with *all* their UV pairs at `FF FF`: **0 of 1158**; the other pairs are regular corners. *Held.* (The first count alone would be weak, see the warning in 258; the second is the one that decides.) |
| 260 | ~~The texture slot table is cumulative across files: a level draws with slots registered by the files loaded before it (the main menu's file, then the area's common file, then the level), so a tool that looks at one `.bze` cannot draw it.~~ **DISPROVED by 275.** `DATA` (the counts stand, the conclusion does not). What was counted: 125 faces of the placed objects' models of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) (the terrain and the templates' models were not counted) reference 8 texture ids that the level does not register; five of them (3, 4, 6, 291, 293) are registered by *Hey... What's up, Dock? 3* (`L03ACOM`), the common file of the dock area, and all eight by *Main Menu* (`TITLE`), which the game opens at start-up; that gave a "complete" table of 456 slots (442 from the level, 10 from `L03ACOM`, 4 from `TITLE`). What stands instead: those 8 ids are **animated texture slots, filled at every tick by objects of the same level** (275): slots 3–7 are the sun's halo, 291/293 Bugs's eyes, 307 Merlin's eye. That other files register the same numbers proved nothing, because almost every file of the disc registers slots 3–7 and 291/293, so any companion file would have "explained" them; and on screen the chain gave a barrel strap in place of the eyes and an olive fan in place of the halo (269). One thing of the old text remains true and harmless: in the code the game registers every texture in a numbered slot (in the other build `0x4229A0`, the TIM registry at `0x52FD60`, 2000 slots of 48 bytes) and no routine clears that table at a level change, unlike the sound registry (O152) — the absence of a clearing routine is a reading of the other build's references to the table, not re-read in 1.0: `HYPOTHESIS`; but nothing on the disc needs it to be drawn. For a tool: one `.bze` is enough; do not load companion files for textures. | The original check ("the 8 missing ids are registered by the files loaded earlier") *held*, **without power**: nearly every file registers those slot numbers. The check that replaced it is 275's: across the whole disc, faces that are drawn and reference an empty slot, once the animated slots are filled from the level's own objects: **0 of 277,588**. |
| 261 | **A model's parts are in local space and are assembled with the rig and the opening pose of an animation, and the transforms are chained along the parents.** `DATA`. The vertices of every part of a multi-part model are local; what puts them in place is the rig (role 4, a `0x50` stream of type 1) and the first pose of an animation (type 2; type 4 too, see 265). In *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) **58 of the 77 placed objects have a multi-part model, and all 58 have a rig**. Ombelll's documents contradict each other on how to compose the transforms: O33 says a key pose gives the full transform of every part, a section of their model-format document says they are concatenated along the parent chain. **On these files the chain wins**, by the same measure the documents use for Bugs: assembled without the chain he is 150 units tall and sits in a heap, with the chain he is **285** units tall, a standing figure. Two details that only show up by trying:<br>• the first block of an animation is not always a pose: for model 112 it holds only "create part" records and leaves the object stacked on the origin; read on through the blocks until every part that has a mesh has received its transform (278 explains why: a block carries only what changes);<br>• which animation to take the pose from: this finding chose the one with the most TRS records (choosing by role number picked a degenerate stream; by count model 112 goes from a heap to a recognisable pirate). That rule is superseded: the right pose is the one the game starts with, see 272.<br>**Its rule for choosing the pose is replaced by 272.** | Bugs's height in `L03A`, the measure Ombelll's documents use: **150 units without the chain, 285 with it**, and only the second is a standing figure: the bar was not written before, and the judgement was by eye against the game's Bugs; the nearest number in the data is his collision box, 400 units tall (300): 285 is of that order, 150 is not; all 58 multi-part objects of the level have a rig to chain on. *Held* for the chain. The "most TRS records" rule was never tested blind and was later replaced (272). |
| 262 | **On the PC's software renderer a UV byte becomes the pixel `u / 255 × (size − 1)`, sampled at the texel centre: not `u / 256`.** `CODE` (Ombelll's reading of `0x41CD50` in the other build; in `bugs.exe` 1.0 the same function is `0x41CDD0`), applied here to the files. Dividing by 256 shifts the picture by half a texel: invisible with a "nearest" filter, visible as soon as linear filtering or mipmaps are used; on a 16×16 texture half a texel is 3% of the surface. Two things this row did not know. First, the reading does not say from which side v is counted: it is from the bottom (271). Second, `0x41CDD0` is called only by the drawers of the **software renderer** (`0x4140B0`, `0x4143E0`): the OpenGL renderer, the one a player normally runs, does not use it. There the coordinate is `byte / 255` over the whole texture with `GL_REPEAT` and `GL_LINEAR` (328), and after a last loop that 328 missed it is `clamp(byte / 255, 0.01, 0.99)` on every OpenGL card (341). For a tool: the (size − 1) rule reproduces the software renderer (and is nearer to the PlayStation); to imitate the PC's OpenGL picture follow 341. **Its v axis is corrected by 271; its (size − 1) rule is the software renderer's: for OpenGL see 328, then 341.** | None on the disc: a reading of the code. The two rules differ by half a texel at each border (1.6% of a 32-pixel texture, 328); the test that can tell them apart is a side-by-side of a small texture's border in the game under each renderer, not run here. |
| 263 | **The 102 `.bmp` files in `Datas/bze` are not previews of the levels: each is a 256 × 1280, 8-bit dump of video memory.** `DATA`. All 102 files are 328,758 bytes and their header declares 256 × 1280 pixels at 8 bits per pixel: the shape of a texture page dump, not of a screenshot. Anyone looking on the disc for a ground truth of how a level looks will not find it there; the only ground truth is the running game. | Reading the header of every file: a preview would have a screen-shaped size and sizes varying with the content. **102 of 102** have the same byte size and the same 256 × 1280, 8-bit header. *Held.* What the pages contain, texture by texture, was not examined. |
| 264 | **The PC executable studied for every finding from 256 on is `bugs.exe` 1.0 of BugsDecomp's version table (772,096 bytes, SHA-256 beginning `74ab71e1`);** Ombelll's documents describe another build of the same size, which behaves as BugsDecomp's 1.6: data addresses are the same, code addresses are not, and the shift is not constant. `CODE`. The `Datas/bin/bugs.exe` of the copy studied here (an installed retail copy) has SHA-256 `74ab71e175a3f186460582cf5b870c2d216ff10d7fedd683b960aa59269f1be4` and MD5 `59731f0354f8a66e63502bd3b521bd82`: the MD5 of **version 1.0** in BugsDecomp's table, so BugsDecomp's C sources and its `PC v1.0` addresses apply to it. It is the build O208 calls the "BBLIT release", **not** the `6e15f920…` build to which every function name and code address of Ombelll's documents belongs. That other build was not available here; it **behaves as BugsDecomp's version 1.6** (MD5 `121DB63D…` in their table; the hash itself could not be compared): the 1.0 − 1.6 differences of BugsDecomp's table are exactly the shifts measured here by reading the code. The level data is identical (`Merlin.bze` has the documented hash, O208).<br>**`.data` is at the same addresses in both builds**: every `DAT_…` of Ombelll's documents applies to 1.0 (the level tables; the condition table `0x4AC550`, the action table `0x4AC6E0`, the type table `0x4AE098`); only the *contents* of tables of code pointers differ.<br>**`.text` is shifted, growing with the address** (address in 1.0 = address in the other build + shift), pairs confirmed by reading what the function does:<br>• `0x401AF0`–`0x401E10` (ADPCM decoders): 0 (candidates)<br>• `0x406190` → `0x4061B0` (start-up): +0x20 (candidate)<br>• from BugsDecomp's table, earlier than the pairs read here show: +0x20 at `0x4079E0`, +0x30 at `0x409A40`, +0x60 at `0x409CE0` (1.0 addresses)<br>• `0x42A7D0` → `0x42A850` (condition dispatcher): +0x80<br>• around `0x42AB20` the shift is **not monotonic**, functions were reordered: +0x50, +0xE0, +0x80<br>• `0x42C6D0` → `0x42C760` (action dispatcher): +0x90 (also the handlers of actions `0x17` and `0x19`)<br>• `0x42E7A0` → `0x42E910` (load script reader; its object dispatch `0x42FDB6` → `0x42FF26`): +0x170<br>• `0x434B00` → `0x434C70` (zone test), `0x436B60` → `0x436CD0` (collision block of a position), `0x440120` → `0x440290` (type 14 handler), `0x447D40` → `0x447EB0` (live list tick), `0x44BB50` → `0x44BCC0` (shadow pass): +0x170, as is everything the documents describe from the loader up to the draw passes<br>• `0x44E310` → `0x44E4B0` (candidate) and the `CreateFileA` call site `0x458665` → `0x458805`: +0x1A0; the IAT slot of `CreateFileA` is `0x009CC310` there and `0x009CC318` in 1.0 (O208).<br>So most of the inserted code sits between `0x42C6D0` and `0x42E7A0`. **A documented address can read cleanly as code in 1.0 and be another function**: `0x434B00` is the zone test in the other build and the object box test in 1.0. For a tool or a Cheat Engine table: check the hash before using any code address; functions reached only through the condition, action and type tables are best found from those tables, which are in `.data`. (N8) | The build identification made predictions that could fail. All 159 `PC v1.0` function addresses of BugsDecomp's table had to be function starts in this executable: **159 of 159** (null 3.6%): *held*. The 100 functions Ombelll's documents name, brought over through the 153 address pairs of that table, had to land on exactly one function start: **93 of 100** (null 15.8%): *held*. The 8 shifts confirmed by reading the code (the pairs above not marked candidate: `0x42A7D0`, `0x42C6D0`, `0x42E7A0`, `0x434B00`, `0x436B60`, `0x440120`, `0x447D40`, `0x44BB50`) all come out of the 1.0 − 1.6 differences exactly. Earlier, O208's own test: tracepoints placed at the documented code addresses land mid-instruction in this build. |
| 265 | **`0x50` streams of type 4 are animations like type 2;** a reader that accepts only type 2 as a pose leaves 35 objects of the dock levels as a heap of parts on their origin. `DATA`. The header of a `0x50` stream carries a type at `+2`: 1 (rig), 2 (animation) and 4. Ombelll's model-format document mentions 4 once, with a question mark. A type 4 stream has the same body as the others: a chain of pose blocks (`u32` record count, `u32` time, then the self-describing records), with ordinary TRS records (107, 36, 181 per stream in the examples looked at) and almost always a box record (type 9) per block: they are the animations of objects that move. What it changes on the dock: counting the objects with a multi-part model, a rig and **no pose** (hence assembled with the identity) gave 35 (10 + 6 + 19 in the three dock files); accepting type 4 brings them to 0, and model 161 is again a standing palm, model 285 a sloping gangway, and the mooring rope stops being a giant plank. The count alone would not have been enough: it is the before and after picture that shows the pose found is the right one. **What distinguishes type 4 from type 2 is not established**: "4 carries a box per block, 2 does not" agrees with the counts and is `HYPOTHESIS` (test: count the box records per block in every type 2 and type 4 stream of the disc; one type 2 stream with a box in every block, or a type 4 without, disproves it). **Explained by 316:** word `+2` is not a type but the frame's length in sixtieths of a second, halved by the loader into ticks (2 = one tick a frame, 4 = two; the rigs carry 1 and are never played; 6, 5 and 30 occur in 53 streams of the disc): a "type 4" stream is an animation at 15 frames a second, and the box-per-block hypothesis is moot. | The block chain had to close on the resource's end, as it does for types 1 and 2; a stream of another kind would overrun or stop short. It closes **exactly on the last byte in 205 type 4 streams of 205**, in *Wabbit on the run! 1* (`L01A`), *Nowhere* (`MERLIN`) and the three files of *Hey... What's up, Dock?* (`L03A`, `L03ACOM`, `L03A2`). *Held.* Objects with a rig and no pose in the dock files: 35 before, 0 after. |
| 266 | ~~A reader that takes the blend mode only from the UV modes draws the semi-transparent `0x4A`/`0x4E` faces opaque by mistake.~~ **REJECTED for the dock, then settled by 307: those faces ARE opaque on the PC.** `DATA` for the counts, `CODE` for what the PC does (307), not compared with the game. The worry: in modes `0x4A`/`0x4E` the word at `+10`, where UV modes keep the blend mode, falls inside the colours, so a reader gets no blend mode for them; if such a face had bit 3 of the flag byte (semi-transparent) it would be drawn opaque without anyone noticing. Measured: in the three files of *Hey... What's up, Dock?* (`L03A`, `L03A2`, `L03ACOM`) **0 faces of 4899** in those modes have bit 3; their flags are only `0x01` and `0x03`. For the dock the worry is void. But over the 53 playable levels the case exists: **6 terrain faces** in *Mine or mine? 2* (`L03C1`) and **20 model faces** (*Train your Brain! 3* (`L05A3C`) 12; *The Carrot Factory 1*, *2*, *4* and *5* (`L04C1`, `L04D1`, `L04D2`, `L04C3`) 2 each; a count that takes a model once per placed object says 30, `L04C3`'s model being placed three times). What stands: the code settles it the other way round. In these modes the blend byte is at `+6`, and the pass that marks which copy of a texture a face needs marks the texture of a `0x4A`/`0x4E` face *opaque only*, without looking at the flag: **the PC draws a semi-transparent `0x4A`/`0x4E` face opaque**, so drawing them opaque is what imitates the PC (307 counts the 20 model faces and did not walk the terrain's: those are 234 on the disc, 6 of them in a playable level). From the same census: modes `0x34` and `0x38` do not appear in the three dock files, and bit `0x02` of the flag byte is set on about a fifth of the faces of every mode: 307 reads it in the code as "double sided" (the backface test is switched off). | The count that could have shown the bug on the dock: **0 of 4899**, so the hypothesis had nothing to bite on there; on the whole disc 234 terrain faces and 20 model faces. The later reading (307) came with a check that *held*: no UV face blends the textures those faces use. What the PlayStation does with them was not looked at. |
| 267 | **The sky dome is not part of the level's geometry: it travels with the camera, so the sky, sun, clouds and sea horizon are always centred on the viewpoint.** `GAME` (compared with the PC game; the pictures are not published). In *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) object 0 (model 1, 231 faces) is placed at (0, 0, 0) and is a closed shell centred on its own origin, ±23,465 units (339): sky, sun, clouds and sea up to the horizon. Drawn fixed at the origin it is wrong: Bugs's starting point is near the edge of the shell and all that showed were two grey tips. Drawn first, without depth, and translated onto the camera, the scene matches the game: **the sun sets behind the stack of crates with the ship's wheel**, which is impossible with the dome fixed in the world. This is the effect players describe as "the background moves with Bugs": the backdrop depends on where you look, never on where you are. Whether the dome also rotates with the view was left open here (the screenshots rule out a full rotation: the sun is not in every direction); the code answers: only the position is taken from the camera, rotation and scale stay the file's (339). The code also names the mechanism: a type 14 object with bit `0x20000000` in the second dword of opcode `0x16` (and every type 9 object) gets, every tick, the camera's position, with Y plus the word of opcode `0x1E`, and is never culled by distance; 36 placed objects on the disc have the bit and 11 of them are not at the origin in the file (339). "Drawn first, without depth" is a simplification that fails where two shells follow the camera: the game resolves them with the ordinary depth buffer (346). **Completed by 339 (the bit that makes an object follow the camera) and 346 (depth, not order, decides between two domes).** | The position of the sun against the scenery, compared with screenshots of the game from known spots: with the dome fixed at the origin the sun cannot be behind the stack of crates with the ship's wheel; with the dome on the camera it is, as in the game. *Held.* |
| 268 | **On the PC the vertex colour multiplies the texture by `byte / 255`:** 255 is neutral and there is no ×2; the PlayStation convention (128 neutral, 255 doubles) does not hold for the port. `GAME` (the pixels of the PC game's picture; the pictures are not published) + `CODE` (306). Corrects O16 for the PC version. Ombelll's documents multiply texture × vertex colour × 2. Compared with screenshots of the PC game in the same framing, a rebuild of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) with ×2 is about twice as bright on everything: a salmon pink hull instead of brown, a bright blue sea instead of navy. The sea is the clean sample, because it is made of `0x4A`/`0x4E` faces with solid-colour 4×4 textures (258). Medians of corresponding zones, (R, G, B):<br>• sea: game (0, 0, 63); with ×2 (0, 0, 125), ratio 0.50; with ×1 **(0, 0, 63)**, ratio 1.00<br>• upper sky: game (32, 58, 88); with ×2 (37, 113, 170), ratios 0.86 / 0.51 / 0.52; with ×1 (18, 57, 85), ratios 1.78 / 1.02 / 1.04<br>• hull: game (89, 52, 41); with ×2 (163, 87, 65), about 0.6; with ×1 (82, 44, 32), about 1.1 to 1.3.<br>The red of the sky and the hull diverge more because the zones are not the same pixel for pixel and the colour varies across the surface, but no zone is compatible with ×2. The code then said the same and explained the rest: the OpenGL renderer uses `GL_MODULATE` with the colour byte turned into `byte / 255` through the table at `0x467B50`, and every texel of every texture goes through a gamma when it is uploaded, `c' = int(256 · (c / 256)^(1 / 1.2))` (32 → 45, 64 → 80, 128 → 143, 200 → 208), which is why the game's hull is 1.1 to 1.3 times the ×1 rebuild (306). "255 doubles" stays right for the PlayStation. **Read in the code by 306.** | The sea of `L03A`, a solid colour, had to match one factor and not the other: game (0, 0, 63), ×1 gives (0, 0, 63), ×2 gives (0, 0, 125). *Held* for ×1, exactly, on the clean sample; sky and hull agree in direction, not pixel for pixel (the hull's residue is 306's gamma). |
| 269 | ~~The sun's halo uses textures that no static file holds: either the load chain of 260 is not the game's, or slots 3–7 are filled in some other way.~~ **CLOSED by 275: they are filled in another way.** `DATA`. The glow around the sun of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) is 10 faces of model 1 (the sky dome, part 2: see 270) with blend mode 3 (B + F/4), an almost uniform yellow vertex colour (about 255, 255, 50) and UVs over the whole texture: the faded shape must come from the texture. The faces name slots **3–7**, which `L03A` does not register. With the cumulative table of 260 those slots came from *Hey... | 274 | **The blue chests, the falling green crates and the flames of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) are not placed objects:** they are templates (block `0x08`, no position) cloned at run time by a rule of a live object. `DATA` for who asks for them and under which condition; `GAME` for their being there (seen in the game on the PC and on the PlayStation, while a rebuild from the placed objects alone showed nothing); `HYPOTHESIS` here for where the chests stand, later read in the code (314). The mechanism is O194's: a `0x31` rule with effect `0x100` or `0x40000` clones the template whose role is in the rule's field `+28`, at the parent's position and rotation. In `L03A`:<br>• **3 blue chests** (model 305, roles 764–766): asked for by object 83, an empty model with a rig; condition level byte 114 == 1, 2 or 3: three rules lay them out in three orders, and other rules assign 1, 2 or 3 with effect `0x2` (a draw of the C runtime's `rand()`, 0…32767: the rule passes when the draw is above its parameter, 25000 for order 1, then 20000 for order 2, else order 3): **the order of the chests is drawn at random**<br>• **8 green crates** (model 311): asked for by 8 triggers on the piers, effect `0x8200100`: target the player, radius 640, one bit of level bytes 106 and 107 per crate; in the game they fall when Bugs gets close<br>• the "little dome" with the propeller (objects 138/139, model 392) is a placed object, and asks for roles 902 (model 88) and 904 (model 95).<br>**Where the chests stand.** Their rule also carries bit `0x80` and its field `+24` is 1, 2 or 3; the rig of object 83 has three child parts at **x = −400, 0, +400**. Read as "`+24` = the k-th child part", the three chests stand in a row, as in a PlayStation screenshot of the game. The code then gave the real rule: with `0x80` the clone is a **child hung from the part the spawner's animation marks at that moment** (record type 10, the attachment marker of O73; the root when nothing is marked), and the rule's first parameter is the frame of birth, not an attach point; "k-th marked part" agrees with it whenever the k-th marker is the one in force at frame k, which is the case for the chests and the torches (279, 314). For a tool: rules are evaluated over time in the game; a static picture can show "what is true at start-up" (conditions true with empty tables, O161, and no target at a distance) or "everything that can appear"; many clones are transient effects (explosion stars, digging holes, carrots jumping out), and the chests, ordered at random at run time, belong to the second set. Left open here: the animated torch was not among these clones (it is found in 279); the little dome comes out semi-transparent from the files (all its 14 faces are in additive blend) while on the PlayStation it looks solid; a "!" high in the sky comes from a start-up clone. **Its placement rule is read in the code by 314.** | For the chests: three child parts of object 83's rig had to exist and be laid out like the chests of the game; they are at x = −400, 0, +400 and the row matches a PlayStation screenshot: *held*, but as a consistent reading, not a blind test (it was not read in the code until 314). For the crates: the rule's target and radius predict "they fall when Bugs comes within 640 units"; in the game they do fall when Bugs gets close (the radius itself was not measured). |

Object 62, near the start, lets you in (within 400) only with bit `0x80` of level byte 102, which object 97 sets in its step 199: the explosive barrel (model 157) sitting on the hole; `GAME` (seen in play on the PC, not measured): the hole opens after the barrel explodes, lit with the torch. In step 199 the barrel clones the explosion (roles 548 and 669, the white star) and its pieces (662), sets the bit and switches itself off (effect `0x10000`). The link with the torch is a `HYPOTHESIS`: state 3 of the barrel has condition `0x0C` (`player+0x17C`) on value 69, perhaps "Bugs holds the torch".<br>**2. Action `0x26`.** The handler is at `0x42D070` (action table at `0x4AC6E0`; handlers `0x17` and `0x19` lie `0x90` after the addresses Ombelll's documents give for the other build) and does `[obj+0xC0]+0x12 += value*16 + index` (the two `s16` at +4 and +6 of the action record). `obj+0xC0` points to `obj+0xCC` (`lea edx,[eax+0xCC]; mov [eax+0xC0],edx`, in the loader at `0x42FEFB`), so the field is `obj+0xDE`, where the loader (`0x430250`) copies the *second* `s16` of the load script's rotation: the Y. The anchor's step 267 has as its first rule `[0x26, 0, 136]` with no condition and with `0x8000` (the walk goes on to the next rule: 366); the type 14 handler walks the step every tick (O188), so the anchor turns by 136/4096 of a turn at every pass. The other rules of the step describe the fall: rule 2 resets the object's clock (action 25) with the player within 300, rule 5 looks for a role 725 (the pirates) within 160, rule 4 changes state when the clock (`0x16`, `obj+0x22`) passes 30. State 501: animations 153 (the shadow grows), 348 (it falls), 198 (on the ground). Speed: one turn is 4096/136 = 30.1 passes; with the rules run once per logic tick, 30 a second (read in the code by 316), that is one turn in 1.004 s, 60 frames of a 60 Hz recording; 120 frames would need the rules to run every other tick, and they do not. Deliberately not a spinner: pirate 110 turns by 60 × 16 = 960 (84°) in each of its four states, and each state ends near a waypoint (roles 58, 139, 140; effect `0x10000000` without `0x8000`): a patrol round.<br>Open: the anchor model is tilted by about 40° in its own plane (not compared with the game); part 4 appears in state 404 (roles 371 and 211) at +398 in z: perhaps the planted anchor.<br>What the record does in the code (347): in the frame applier (`0x44E5A0`, the `0xB` case at `0x44E8A2`) it zeroes the part's scale and part of its local matrix and sets bit `0x8000` of the part's flag word, so the part is hidden, with no shadow, not deleted; any TRS record for the part clears the bit (`0x44E6CA`), and since the scale was zeroed the TRS that brings it back must carry the scale again. **Read in the code by 347.** | If the record takes the part out of the pose, the part that comes back must be set again from scratch. After a type 8 / `0xB` record the first TRS of the part is complete (flag `0xE`) in **638 cases out of 638**, against 2443 out of about 249,000 (about 1%) for an arbitrary TRS, which usually carries only the rotation (flag 2): held. For action `0x26`: a reading of the code. In the game: time one turn of an anchor of `L03A` on a 60 Hz recording; 60 frames predicted, 120 would show the rules running every other tick; not run. |

What's up, Dock? 2*, see 344: 1029 points, then 136 normal sectors).<br>• Without a header the sector's polygons run up to the next header or point block: the polygon modes (`0x34`–`0x4E`) cannot be mistaken for the header modes (`0x00`–`0x10`) nor for `0x44`.<br>• Effect: `CCEND` builds (605 faces); `CCMERLIN` and `L03A2_8` gave zero terrain faces, because the points were taken for a header and the rest discarded; read correctly they give 2514 and 3059. | 290 | **The game's zone test: the distance is taken in 16 bits, Y is never rotated, bit 0 of `zone+8` means "no rotation at all", bit 1 "any area";** so every zone acts again 65,536 units away on each axis. `CODE`, plus a reproduction checked on the disc; not tested in the game. The function that finds the zone of a point is at `0x434C70` (Ombelll's documents give it as `0x434B00`: that is the other build, see 264; in this build `0x434B00` is the object box test). It walks the zone list at `0x4B3238`, and for each zone:<br>• skips the comparison of the area number if **bit 1 of `zone+8`** is set;<br>• d = position − origin, **in 16 bits** (`sub word`);<br>• Y **without rotation**: `y_limit <= d.y` and `d.y + box.y <= 0`, where `box.y` is the word at `object+0x52` of the object's box (so a zone counts Bugs's box, not only his origin: measured in the game in 299);<br>• if **bit 0 of `zone+8`** is set (700 of 1147 zones), X and Z without rotation: `0 <= d.x <= extent X`, `0 <= d.z <= extent Z`;<br>• otherwise d is turned by `RotMatrixZYX` of the negated angles (`0x1000 − a`; the matrix of O105, signs as in BugsDecomp's `gte.c`) and the same test is made on x' and z'. The comparison is signed: a negative extent never finds anything.<br>The flag words are opcode `0x16` of the zone (O103); a tool must keep them. Of the zones that kill, teleport or hurt only two carry a real rotation: a death zone of *Train your Brain! 4* (`L05A4`) (315°, but with X extent −31787: disabled, the "unreachable" zone of O103) and a **damage zone of *What's cookin', Doc? 5* (`L02A4`)** (341°, around Y), the only one whose shape changes. | 305 | **How the game draws a world sprite: the box is twice the `0x64` record and the object's scale is applied twice (width = 2·w·sx², height = 2·h·sy²),** the bottom edge at the object's origin unless a bit centres it; and the PC's OpenGL renderer has no additive mode: every semi-transparent texel is alpha-blended towards its own colour pushed to full brightness. `CODE` (the scene loop `0x44C620`, the sprite instance `0x438AE0`, the projection `0x4383F0`, the queueing `0x44C2E0`, the OpenGL renderer `0x40A720` and `0x411BA0` (one address corrected by 306: the flush is `0x412540`), texture upload `0x422AC0`, `0x423F30`, `0x420D90`); the equality of units with the models in Y is a `HYPOTHESIS`; no comparison with a screenshot was made here (see 338 and 345).<br>**Which instances are sprites**: the instance's kind word (`instance+8`): bits 2 and 4 a model (renderer callback `[0x7C9618]`), **bits `0x11` a sprite** (bit `0x10` a screen sprite with colour `0x808080` or a fade, bit 1 a world sprite), `0x20` a texture slot animator, `0x40` a box.<br>**Size.** Making the instance, the game sets the object's box from the `0x64` record (width `+0xC`, height `+0xE`) **times the object's scale** (opcode `0x12`; `+0xCC` X, `+0xD0` Y): x and z from -(w/2)·sx to +(w/2)·sx, top `+0x52` = -h·sy, bottom 0. The projection hides a sprite nearer than 150 units, takes `k = 400 / depth` (the same H = 400 as the models' perspective `0x408E30`, set by `0x40CD80`) and spans the screen from `sx + 2·box.xmin·k·scaleX` to `sx + 2·box.xmax·k·scaleX`, and in Y `2·box.top·k·scaleY` times the aspect word `0x4EFB5C`: the box is doubled and **the scale applied a second time**. In the units in which a model W wide spans W·400 / depth: **width = 2·w·sx², height = 2·h·sy²** (X has no aspect factor on either side; for Y this assumes the aspect word scales models alike, not read; read by 315).<br>**Anchor.** The bottom edge is at the object's origin and the sprite rises by its height (`0x43856C`): the reading adopted in 279, here read in the code. **With `object+0xC & 0x8000000` the sprite is centred on the origin** (`0x438545`); with `0x4000000` its position is lowered by h/2 when made. (`object+0xC` is the second dword of the object's opcode `0x16`: 338.)<br>**The torch of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`).** Flame (textures 218 to 222, record 32 × 64, scale 4505, 4915 = 1.1, 1.2, opaque): **77 × 184**, resting on the top of the torch. Glow (texture 250, record 64 × 64, scale 8192, 8192 = 2, 2, mode 1, `+0xC = 0x8000000`): **512 × 512, centred**. A tool that draws the record's size with no scale, resting on its point, draws the flame 2.4 times too narrow and 2.9 too short and the glow at an eighth of its diameter and 256 units too high. This is the game's shared code, not the renderer: it should hold for the PlayStation too (`HYPOTHESIS`: nothing was read in the PlayStation's executable for it).<br>**Colour.** World sprites get the neutral colour `0x808080` and the OpenGL callback writes 1.0 to every colour component of the four corners: unlit, full texture colour, whatever the level's lighting.<br>**Blending, OpenGL renderer** (renderer word `0x4AC094 & 2`). All semi-transparent polygons go in one list drawn with one blend function (`0x413011`: `glEnable(GL_ALPHA_TEST)`, `glEnable(GL_BLEND)`, `glBlendFunc`): with the word at `0x4AC030` equal to 0, `GL_SRC_ALPHA, GL_ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA`; otherwise `GL_ONE, GL_SRC_COLOR` with an alpha of 0.5 for every mode (`0x42298A`; who sets `0x4AC030` was not read; read by 306). The PlayStation's four modes differ only in the **alpha built when the texture is uploaded**: a pass over every model (`0x423F30`) marks each texture with the modes it is used with (bits 1, 2, 4, 8, and `0x10` opaque, at `0x52FD84 + 48·n`), the loader makes **one copy of the texture per mode** (`0x422E8A`) and converts each palette entry (`0x420D90`; 5 bits shifted to 8; m = the largest of R, G, B):<br>word 0: alpha 0;<br>mode 0 (B/2 + F/2): alpha m / 2, each channel + (255 - m);<br>mode 1 (B + F): alpha m - m / 4, each channel + (255 - m);<br>mode 2 (B - F): alpha 0.299 R + 0.587 G + 0.114 B, colour unchanged;<br>mode 3 (B + F/4): alpha m / 4, each channel + (255 - m);<br>opaque: alpha 255, colour unchanged.<br>(A grey entry becomes white with the same alpha.) So `out = back + alpha · (pushed - back)`: it cannot exceed the pushed colour and it **darkens** a background brighter than it, where the PlayStation's addition only brightens. The glow's centre texel (120, 104, 8) becomes (255, 239, 143) with alpha 90 of 255: over (20, 24, 40) the screen shows **(103, 100, 76), a pale yellowish haze**, where an addition gives (140, 128, 48); over a bright (120, 110, 90): (168, 156, 109) against (240, 214, 98). 2414 of its 4096 texels are drawn, mean alpha 41 of 255. The other sprite callback (`0x40AB50`) belongs to the software renderer (not a DirectX one: N8) and was not read; the PlayStation adds for real. For a tool: choose between the PlayStation's addition and the PC's converted alpha; they look different. Flat modes repeat one colour; world sprites are white (305). While a screen fade runs every colour is multiplied by its level (`0x467740`). <br>**Texture gamma.** Every texel colour of every texture, opaque ones too, goes through a table built at upload (`0x4B5B40`): `c' = int(256 · (c/256)^g)`, **g = 1/1.2 on OpenGL** (renderer selector `0x4AC094` = 2), **1/1.7 on the 24-bit software renderer**, none on the 8-bit one; then through a contrast and brightness table (`0x424F70`: `c · contrast + brightness`), the identity with the default configuration (`config+0x70` = 1.0, `config+0x78` = 0). The switch `0x4AC034` is on for every OpenGL driver profile. Dark and middle texels come out brighter than the file's: 32 → 45, 64 → 80, 128 → 143, 200 → 208; vertex colours are not touched. With it the torch glow's example of 305, (103, 100, 76), becomes (103, 101, 81) on screen. For a tool: apply the table to the texture's RGB when it is uploaded, not after the vertex colour. <br>**Driver profiles (`0x4220D0`).** The vendor and renderer strings are searched for `3Dfx`, `NEC`, `RENDITION`, `ATI` / `RAGE PRO`, `NVIDIA` / `RIVA`, `Real3D`, `Direct3D`. Every profile but `Direct3D` (a wrapper) and the software one sets `0x4AC030` = 0: `glBlendFunc(GL_SRC_ALPHA, GL_ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA)` and face alpha 1.0 on any modern card, so the conversion of 305 (the alpha built into a copy of the texture per blend mode) holds for every semi-transparent face, models and terrain. With `Direct3D` in the renderer string: `GL_ONE, GL_SRC_COLOR`, alpha 0.5, alpha test on. A semi-transparent face whose texture has no copy for its mode is drawn opaque (307). <br>**The order of a frame (`0x412540`):** <br>1. clear <br>2. untextured triangles and quads <br>3. per texture, in texture order, the opaque faces, with no blending and no alpha test, **except the textures with a transparent palette entry** (byte `+9` of the texture record, set by `0x420D90` when a palette word is 0: cut-outs such as leaves, grilles, the torch's flame): those wait <br>4. alpha test and blending on: the untextured blended faces <br>5. every face of the blended lists and of the waiting cut-out textures is put in one array with the mean view depth of its vertices and sorted **farthest first** (an exchange sort at `0x41353E`), then drawn one by one with blending, **alpha test off**, depth writes on. <br>So the PC sorts its semi-transparent faces back to front per face, and its cut-outs have soft bilinear edges, not alpha-tested ones. <br>**Draw distance and its fade (`0x40CCE0`).** D = 4·d + bonus, at most 65,535. d is the object's own word (`object+0x3E`, opcode `0x3A`) when its culling flags have bit 2 (294), else the level's word at `bugs.exe+0xB38A0` (written by the loader at `0x42FB72`: the third word of opcode `0x3D` of the header block `0x3B`, stored divided by 4; see 308). bonus = (9·s/2) × the level's stored word, s being the game's **fog distance setting** (`config+0x1C`: 0 near, 1 normal, 2 far, the default): 0, 4 or 9 times. Nine modes (index table `0x412504`): `0x34`, `0x38`, `0x3C`, `0x40`, `0x48`, `0x4A`, `0x4C`, `0x4E` are drawn, `0x64` is skipped (16 bytes a record), **any other mode ends the part**. <br>**The nine modes** (record size; vertex indices in the code's order; UV; colours; texture id; blend mode byte): <br>`0x34` flat triangle: 24 bytes; vertices **+20, +22, +14**; UV +4, +8, +12; one colour at +16; texture +6; blend byte +10 <br>`0x38` flat quad: 28 bytes; vertices +20…+26; UV +4, +8, +12, +14; one colour at +16; texture +6; blend byte +10 <br>`0x3C` Gouraud triangle: 32 bytes; vertices +28, +30, +14 (277); UV +4, +8, +12; colours +16, +20, +24; texture +6; blend byte +10 <br>`0x40` Gouraud quad: 40 bytes; vertices +32…+38; UV +4, +8, +12, +14; colours +16…+28; texture +6; blend byte +10 <br>`0x4A` triangle: 28 bytes; vertices +20, +22, +24; no UV, the whole texture; colours +8, +12, +16; texture **+4**; blend byte **+6** <br>`0x4E` quad: 32 bytes; vertices +24…+30; no UV; colours +8…+20; texture +4; blend byte +6 <br>`0x48` (24 bytes) and `0x4C` (28 bytes): vertices from +16 / +20; no UV; colours from +4; a fixed texture from a run-time table; no blend byte <br>`0x64`: 16 bytes, skipped by the drawer (it is the frame record of sprites and animated textures). <br>The texture id goes through the slot table `0x4E5E80`, the one the texture animators write (313). **Mode `0x34`** has the same turn as `0x3C` (277): a reader that takes (+14, +20, +22) keeps the winding and turns the texture by one corner, on 50 faces of the disc. <br>**Rejection and culling.** `0x416E90` (triangles) and `0x416C10` (quads) drop a face whose vertices are all behind the camera, all beyond the draw distance, or all off one side of the screen (306). `0x40FE50` is the backface culling: a face is dropped when the screen cross product (d0 − d1) × (d2 − d1), in the code's vertex order, is negative; **bit `0x02` of the flag byte (`+2`) switches the test off**. The bit is tested once per batch, on its first record. The side kept: with the code's order the normal (v0 − v1) × (v2 − v1), in file coordinates, points out of the part: that is the visible side. The terrain goes through the same routines (312). <br>**Semi-transparency (`0x414620`, `0x414BD0`).** Bit `0x08` of the flag byte; the mode is bits 5–6 of the blend byte; the face takes the copy of its texture made for that mode (305) and alpha `0x467730[mode]` = 1.0. If the texture has no copy for that mode the face is drawn opaque. The marking pass `0x423F30` marks a texture named by a `0x4A` / `0x4E` face as *opaque only*, overwriting and without looking at the flag: **a semi-transparent `0x4A` / `0x4E` face is drawn opaque by the PC**, unless a UV face blends the same texture in the same mode, which happens nowhere on the disc. The same as snapping the copy to the original when the rig brings both to one point, half the gap otherwise. <br>**Quads** are sent as `GL_QUADS` in the order slot 2, 3, 1, 0 of the record (`0x412ED7`): with the Z order of 257 that is the loop v2, v3, v1, v0; OpenGL drivers split it from the first vertex into (v2, v3, v1) and (v2, v1, v0), **the diagonal v1–v2**, the PlayStation's. <br>**Hidden parts.** A part whose instance byte `+0xB4` is 0 is skipped whole: its vertices only advance the counter (`0x411B60`). Nothing else in the renderer hides a face. The second word, "last", is never read.** On the disc the cycles are written from 0 (0…n−1) or **from 1 (1…n)**: a tool that plays first…last never shows frame 0 of a cycle written from 1 and leaves frames out when "last" is short of the end: **47 of 129 animators** would show other frames than the game, which always shows them all. <br>**Type 2 (`0x445750`)** is a state machine like type 14's (O188, O189), without movement: <br>it **starts in state number 2 if the object has one, else number 1** (`0x445797`) <br>it takes the state's first slot and its step, and plays the step's animation (payload `+2` of opcode `0x30`) through the animation executor `0x438AE0` <br>when the animation ends it goes to the state's next slot (five at most, `0xFFF0` ends) <br>its rules (opcode `0x31`) move it to other states: effect `0x40` "the frame equals the second parameter" (completed by 315: the comparison is with the dword at payload `+24`), `0x2` and `0x20000` a random draw against a parameter (`0x4312C0`), a condition, an action; effect `0x4000000` deletes the object whose id is the third parameter, `0x10000` deletes itself. <br>254 of 391 have one state and one step; **137 have up to 14 states** (Bugs's eyes, 14: the blinks and the looks). `0x02` a sound bank (`sound_bank_load` `0x41E160`); `0x04` a catalogue entry (`0x41DEC0`); `0x44` goes to an empty stub `0x42D400` (nothing on the PC); **`0x4C` / `0x4D` six (offset, size) pairs, one per language** (`config+0x10050`, 0…5): the language's speech header (`0x41E0F0`) and bank. <br>**Block `0x05`, opcode `0x06`** (3 bytes, up to 40 entries, table `0x4B3A20`): the music per area; `0x41D980`, called by the game loop, looks up the player's area (`+0x26`) and changes track when the area changes; 72 files. <br>**Block `0x47`, opcode `0x48`** (offset, size → `0x4B249C`, flag `config+0x1004F`): **a recorded input stream**, 8 bytes a tick (four pad words), which `input_build_words` `0x449615` plays back instead of the pad: a demo. **No file of the disc has one**; For each object of the chain that matches the mask on its first flag word, has solid class 2, is not the mover nor tied to it as parent or child (nor a child of Bugs), and whose plan distance is within the sum of the two radii and contact radii (**16-bit differences**):<br>1. the mover's position plus the delta so far is brought into the box's own frame; the two boxes must overlap in Y<br>2. **the mover is five points**: the four corners of its own box pushed out by its contact radius, and a fifth<br>3. each point is given **the side of the box it belongs to by the angle from the box's centre to the point** against the four corner directions (`0x433C35`…): four sectors, +Z, +X, −Z, −X in the box's frame<br>4. if the point after the move is inside the box **grown by the object's contact radius** (opcode `0x38`) on that side, the delta's component across that side is **replaced** so that the point lands on the grown face (delta = face ± R − point); a box with `+8 & 0x2000000` only reports, it does not stop. Each of the five points overwrites the same component: **the last one decides**. A point that starts inside the grown box is thrown out through the face of its own sector, by whatever it takes, in one tick: near the long axis of a long thin box that is the far end of the box, and past the middle plane it is the other side<br>5. no point inside: a second test catches a corner of the box poking into the mover's outline and shortens the delta along the mover's edge (`0x433EC9`…`0x434212`)<br>6. **two boxes in one tick** (`0x434242`): if an earlier object of the chain has already stopped the mover this tick and the present box has not `+8 & 0x20000`, **the delta becomes the mean of the delta so far and the new one**; a box with `0x20000`, or the first box met, replaces it whole. **No box is looked at twice.**<br>**The chain's order**, fixed by the visibility pass that builds it every tick: type 21 objects, then the objects with `+8 & 0x20000` (the hard boxes: walls, gates; they also stop the camera), then the ones that can be stood on (`+8 & 8`), then Bugs, then the rest in reverse list order. So a hard box is always resolved before a soft one, and the soft one's correction, halved, is added after it with nothing to stop it.<br>**Off-screen objects.** Bit `0x20` of the culling word (the screen test) drops an object from the chain (not solid, not run) only when it has neither `0x40` **nor `0x80`** and its `+4 & 2` is clear; `+4` = 2 is written for every object at load (`0x430841`) and for every clone (`0x448D20`) and cleared only when the object is culled; nothing sets it again. So an object is immune to the screen test until another test (distance, area) has culled it once.<br>***Follow the Red Pirate Road*, the data.** The boulder of the magic door is **an object box, not terrain**: object 37 at (20440, −1499, 4745), box 140 × 952 × 1700 (thin in X, long in Z), contact radius 150, flags `0x20380`: hard. Following, with flag 2 clear: the target is Bugs's heading + `0x800` (behind him), the angle moves by error × 30 / 2048 a tick, and when that rounds to 0 flag 2 is set; a countdown of 60 ticks (`0x4B3E28`) is started by some events before following resumes (its writers were not read: the word is addressed, the events are not). **The heading search**, every tick while flag 2 is clear: from the current angle it tries **24 offsets in this order: 0, +341, −341, +682, −682, +1023, −1023, … +3751, −3751, 4092** (30° apart); for each it sweeps a vector of the camera's length from Bugs's place with the wall sweep (`0x434E40`); **the first offset whose sweep is neither cut nor flagged wins** (when all 24 fail, the longest), and **a quarter of the winning offset is added to the angle each tick** (`0x4329A3`). What cuts the sweep: a solid collision cell (`0x7F`), ground more than 100 above the ray's own height (the camera's Y, so only tall things), and **the side of the collision block the ray starts in, where no block continues at that height**. Object boxes are not in this search; they act later on the camera's position: `0x431510` (mask `0x24000`: `+8 & 0x4000` or `0x20000`) moves the camera's end point onto the grown face of the box of the sector its start point belongs to, which can be a face on the other side of Bugs, and next tick the angle is measured from there (the way object 68 turns the camera). **`HYPOTHESIS`, until it is logged in the game: a flip is what happens when every heading from 0 to ±150° is shut and 180° is the first free one (+2046 is tried before −2046): the angle then moves 511 a tick and the camera is on the other side in four ticks.** Smaller swings are the same with a nearer free heading: 85, 170, 256, 341, 426 a tick. Places: Bugs close to a side of his collision block with the camera on the outer side (an invisible border: nothing is seen there), a concave corner, an alcove, a tall step behind him. The PC's camera length is at least `0x5DC` = 1500 (`0x432517`); why the PlayStation may flip less is not readable here.<br>**What of the game's logic depends on the camera:** the chunk drawn and the list of visible areas start from the camera's collision block; the visibility pass decides who runs and who is in the ordering chain, that is **who is solid for Bugs and for every mover**; an object with the screen test and neither `0x40` nor `0x80`, once culled, stops being solid as soon as it is off screen, so turning the camera can switch off the collision of such objects behind it (hard scenery is flagged `0x40` and rudders and carried things `0x80` in the objects of this level looked at (not counted over the disc)). For a tool: mark the hard boxes (`0x20000`) apart from the soft ones; the sides of the area boxes and the hard walls are where flips come from. So on the window x = 400 × (W / 512) × X / Z over a half-width of W / 2 and y = 400 × (H / 384) × Y / Z over a half-height of H / 2: tan(half the horizontal angle) = 256 / 400 = 0.64, **65.24° across**; tan(half the vertical angle) = 192 / 400 = 0.48, **51.28° up and down**; 0.64 / 0.48 = 4 / 3, so a window of another shape stretches the picture, it does not widen it. The same numbers come from the PlayStation side (512 wide, 240 rows of 1.6-tall pixels, aspect 0.625), as they must. **The near plane is at depth H / 2 = 200 units** (`0x4EFB38` = −H / 2, the value the sector culling and the model drawer `0x411BA0` compare with): what is nearer to the camera is cut. The OpenGL face queues index them with the face's UV bytes, straight into the queued vertex; the faces without UV (`0x4A`, `0x4E`) and the sprites (`0x40A803`…) take the entries 0 and 255. So **the coordinate is byte / 255: 0 is the outer edge of the first texel, 255 the outer edge of the last**, before the clamp that 341 found. **Wrap and filter** (`0x423732`…): `GL_REPEAT` on both axes, `GL_LINEAR` both ways, no mipmaps (306): this stands. What this row concluded from it, that at a coordinate of exactly 0 or 1 every sprite and every face reaching 0 or 255 shows a half-texel line of **the opposite edge of the same texture**, is what 341 corrects: after the clamp the opposite edge is mixed in only on small textures (nothing from a side of 50 texels up). **The driver profiles** as read here (the words are looked for in the driver's vendor and renderer strings, case kept):<br>`ATI` or `RAGE PRO` (a current Radeon says "ATI Technologies Inc."): **U and V clamped to [4.5 / 255, 250.5 / 255] = [0.017647, 0.982353]**: the outer strip of every texture is never shown: this stands;<br>`RENDITION`, `NEC`, `Direct3D`: that clamp on V only (on U see 341);<br>`3Dfx` (largest texture 256, made square), `NVIDIA` / `RIVA`, `Real3D`, the software and the default profile (no word found: any Intel card): read here as "clamped to [0, 1], that is nothing": **wrong for all but the software profile, see 341**.<br>**Finding 262's rule** (pixel = u / 255 × (size − 1), sampled at the texel centre) is the function at `0x41CDD0` in this build (`0x41CD50` in the other build), whose only callers are `0x4140B0` and `0x4143E0`: no OpenGL call in them, float-to-int conversions and the rasteriser's function pointers (`0x5A7CA8`…): the **software renderer**. The two rules differ by half a texel at each border (1.6% of a 32-pixel texture) and by the wrap. **Texture size** (`0x423510`): the width in pixels is the TIM's words × 4, × 2, × 1 or × 2 / 3 by its mode (4, 8, 16, 24 bits); width and height are each brought down to **the largest power of two not above them**, or, with the configuration byte `0x9CA877` set (not identified: the last byte of the block that begins with the key bindings), to the next one up; at most the value at `0x4AC024` (512; 256 on `3Dfx`). The conversion `0x420D90` fills that size, so the coordinates still span the whole picture. On the disc 298 of 24,675 textures (1.2%) have a side that is not a power of two (216 of them the 20 × 16 HUD digits; 64 × 96, 128 × 120, 96 × 96, 256 × 192…): they are shrunk (96 → 64, 120 → 64, 192 → 128) and should look softer on the PC (a prediction, not seen: `HYPOTHESIS`). For a tool: to look like the PC's OpenGL renderer use byte / 255 with the clamp of 341, repeat wrapping, the linear filter and no mipmaps; the (size − 1) rule is the PC's software renderer's, nearer to the PlayStation's. Completes 262 and 306. (N29) **Corrected by 341** (every OpenGL profile clamps the texture coordinate to 0.01 … 0.99: the bare 0 and 1 of this row do not stand; the rest does). | None on the disc for the rule: a reading of the code, and that reading missed the last loop over the two tables (at `0x4228F4`), which 341 found; the texture count (298 of 24,675) is a count on the level files. In the game, by screenshot: the prediction written for this row (a thin line of the opposite side's colour at the border of every sprite that spans a whole texture, absent on an AMD card) is replaced by 341's; neither was run. |

| 333 | **One pass of the game loop is one logic tick and one picture, and inside it the camera moves first, from where Bugs was at the end of the previous tick;** then visibility, then every handler, then the drawing. Only a handful of the 256 level bytes are touched by the executable itself. `CODE` for the order of the calls (game loop `0x406600`, the walk of the live objects `0x447EB0`); the functions named only by their look were not entered. The order, 30 times a second (316):<br>1 · the tick counter `0x4B24A4` += 2 (it counts 60ths), its step since the last pass to `0x4B2384`<br>2 · the pad words of this tick are built (`0x449330`), or taken from a recorded stream: that function holds a recorder and a player of pad input (mode word `0x4B1D04`: 1 writes the two raw pad bytes of every tick to a buffer, 2 reads them back); who sets the mode was not read. Of interest for TAS work<br>3 · the walk of the live objects: **first the camera update `0x431A60`** (called at `0x447EE7`), then the visibility pass (293 to 297), then every object's handler in list order, Bugs included, each walking its rules once, then the deletions (311)<br>4 · sound (`0x41EE30`, `0x41E330`)<br>5 · unless a level change is pending (`0x4B39BC`): the save service `0x4071B0`, a small state machine driven by level byte 10 as the command and 8 as the result; it reads the index of the saves and fills level bytes 43 … 54 and 59 … 64 for the load menu (`0x407710`); nothing for a level<br>6 · the HUD (`0x449ED0`, `0x449EF0`), unless level byte 0 is not 0, or the pause / dialogue mode of level byte 12 is on, or the film bars (`config+0x10076`), or level byte 23, or `0x4B3940 & 0x300` without bit 1; the music by area (`0x41D980`)<br>7 · the picture: sprites begin (`0x40A590`), the two view matrices from the camera (`0x40CF10`, 327), frame begin (`0x40A250`), the HUD's overlay pass `0x44B4E0`, the scene `0x44C620`, the shadows `0x44BCC0`, the texts `0x44D110`, the buffer swap, the frame pacing `0x406F00`.<br>During a fade or a level change the loop **calls itself** between fade steps (`0x40698D`, `0x406BAB`, `0x406BD9`, `0x406D19`; the fade steps are `0x44C000`, `0x44C060`): the world keeps ticking while the screen fades. For glitches: the camera always lags Bugs by one tick, and the area the visibility pass uses is the one computed before Bugs moved in this tick. | 334 | **Condition `0x34` means "Bugs is diving": the objects that change level on it are rabbit holes.** `CODE` for the handler; the counts on the disc are `DATA`. Corrects the name given in 329 and 331, where the condition was only "bit `0x4` of Bugs's second flag word (`+0x18`)". Every tick the player's handler (`0x43AECC` … `0x43AF24`) copies two bits of **the control dword of his current animation step** into his second flag word: step bit `0x1000` → flag `0x2`, **step bit `0x2000` → flag `0x4`** (with a camera request too, `0x4B3242` = 5 and `0x4B3243` = `0x50`, when his `+0x1B4` is set); without the step bit the flag is cleared. Conditions `0x33` and `0x35` read the first flag, `0x34` and `0x36` the second. On the disc **no step of Bugs has bit `0x1000`**: condition `0x33`, used 63 times, can never pass. **Exactly one series has bit `0x2000`: series 30 (animation role 28), in 45 files**, entered by the action button (circle) in the air, with his own flag `0x8` or `0x200`: it is **the dive into a rabbit hole** that the tutorial's text 220 teaches ("Press the action key while jumping … to dive into a rabbit hole"). So the level exits of 329 that wait for condition `0x34` are **rabbit holes: a type 14 object that changes level, and sets the save byte that picks the arrival (326), when Bugs dives within its reach**. For a tool: a rabbit hole that leads to another level can be told from the others by its rule (condition `0x34`, effect `0x80000`) and labelled with the file it leads to (N34, addendum). | None on the disc beyond the two counts (no step with `0x1000`; one series with `0x2000`, in 45 files): a reading of the code. In the game: bit `0x4` of Bugs's second flag word (`+0x18` of his object) must be set during the dive and at no other time; not run. |

| 337 | **A zone rule has an addressee before it has a condition:** the dword at `+0` of its record is the id of the object the rule is for (1 = Bugs), and the dword at `+4` a mask on that object's flags. What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) moves nobody is also `GAME`. Completes 326.<br>**The whole record: opcode `0x33` of a zone block, 32 bytes, little endian** (offset · size · meaning):<br>`+0` · u32 · **the addressee: the id of the object the rule is for** (1 = Bugs)<br>`+4` · u32 · **a mask on the object's first flag word (`object+0x14`)**; 0 = no mask<br>`+8` · u8 · the condition's number (0 = always)<br>`+9` · u8 · the condition's value `a`<br>`+10` · u8 · the condition's byte index `b`<br>`+11` · u8 · the action's number (0 = none)<br>`+12` · u8 (u16 for the word-valued actions of 331) · the action's value<br>`+14` · u16 · the action's index<br>`+16` · u32 · the effects (326)<br>`+20`, `+24`, `+28` · 3 × s32 · x, y, z, or a number (a LevID, a series) in `+20`.<br>**How the game uses the first two fields** (`0x438652` … `0x438686`), before the condition is even looked at:<br>1 · the object in the zone gives its id **as a 16-bit word** (`object+0x1C`, zero-extended: `and eax, 0xFFFF` at `0x43866F`) and it is compared with **the whole 32-bit dword at `+0`** (`0x438674`); not equal: the rule is skipped<br>2 · if the mask is not 0 it must share a bit with `object+0x14`; else the rule is skipped<br>3 · then the condition (`0x42A850`), the action, the effects.<br>So a zone rule says "when object N is in here": the rules for Bugs carry 1, the recovery nets carry the id of the thing they put back, and so on. The effects `0x800` (teleport), `0x100000` (restart point) and `0x80000` (level change) always act on Bugs, whoever the addressee is; `0x40000000` and `0x10000` act on the object in the zone. If the object's id or the rule's id is 6 and `object+0xC & 2`, the zone is skipped whole: not followed. **On the disc** (2,397 zone rules): **1,847 for Bugs, 548 for another object, 2 for an id over 65535; the mask is 0 in every rule.** The largest object id of the disc is 1364.<br>**The two dead rules.** Zone 12 of `L03A`, origin (17810, −1650, 15680), where Bugs starts, has a restart point (effect `0x100000`) for id 1 and a teleport (`0x800`, condition 0) to (30370, −1200, 6346) **for id 100000 = `0x186A0`**; zone 0 of its twin, *Hey... What's up, Dock? 2* (`L03A2`), the same, to (23319, −1260, 19949). No 16-bit id can equal 100000: the rules never fire, whatever their condition, which is what is seen in the game. The condition bytes really are 0: nothing is misread there. They look like a designer's shortcut across the level (each leads far from the start, the first onto objects and not terrain), switched off by an id out of reach rather than deleted. For a tool: read the addressee and the mask; show "for Bugs" or "for object id N" before the condition; a rule whose addressee is over 65535 is dead: draw no teleport for it (the disc then has 10 teleports, not 12); a rule whose addressee is not 1 acts when that object is in the zone, not Bugs (N37). | Three checks on the disc, bars before the run (the first two written after the two rules were known, the third after a survey). Every other rule id is the id of an object or template of its own file: 359 of 401 (an id counted once per zone) = **89.5% against 90%: *missed by little*** (by chance, against the next file's objects, 16.0%). In the game: standing in zone 12 of `L03A` moves nobody, as read. |
| 338 | **The glow of the torch is one white quad drawn once with its texture's mode 1 conversion: nothing in the code makes it stronger.** The one thing a renderer can get wrong is its centring, a bit that is not in the sprite record but in the object: `0x8000000` of the second dword of opcode `0x16`. `CODE`; the comparison with a video is `HYPOTHESIS`. The question: on a frame of a PC speedrun video the glow of the torch of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) looked far stronger than the faint warm haze the data gives (middle texel (255, 241, 154), alpha 93), and 30 pixels from its middle the blue fell while red and green rose. Completes 305 and 307.<br>**Four leads, each followed in the code: all four are a no.**<br>1 · **Drawn more than once: no.** The scene loop hands each visible sprite instance to the callback once (`0x44C2E0` → `[0x7C624C]` = `0x40A720`); the callback adds one record to the list of its texture; the flush (`0x4137D3` … `0x413982`) draws each record of the blended sprite lists (`0x4E7DCC + 16 × texture`) as one `GL_QUADS` of four vertices (the code calls `glBegin(GL_QUADS)` once before the loop and once more inside it: the extra calls are OpenGL errors, ignored, the same pattern is at `0x41303C`; not a second drawing). In the data each torch has one glow: the torch, template id 671, clones id 670 as its held child at frame 1, and 670 clones **id 660, the glow** (template 76), the same way; a second family, 861 → 910 → **914** (template 229), is the same glow for other torches. It is not in the `0x64` record, whose dword at `+0xC` is width and height. With the bit the screen height is split half above and half below the object's point (`0x438545`); without it the sprite rises from its point. Both glow templates of `L03A` have opcode `0x16` = `00 00 00 02` then `00 00 00 08`: **centred**; the flame (template 66) has all zeros. For a tool: a renderer that rests the glow on its point draws its middle 256 units too high, and beside and under the torch shows the rim of the texture where the game shows its middle; this is the one difference the code gives. 108 of the 1,293 sprite objects and templates of the disc have the bit (against 22 of 8,922 others)<br>· **A sprite is drawn with its texture's own id, not with the copy made for its mode.** The faces look the copy up (`0x4149CA`: `0x52FD74[(texture × 12 + mode) × 4]`, 307); the sprite callback queues under the id in the record (`0x40A7C4`) and the flush binds that id (`0x41380A`). Opaque sprites (list `0x4E7DC4`) go with the opaque faces<br>· The software renderers (`0x4AC094` 0 or 1) treat a mode 1 texture in a way of their own after the conversion (`0x421160` …, not read).<br>**What the video's numbers can say** (`HYPOTHESIS`; a frame scaled 2×, not lossless). | 341 | **On the PC's OpenGL path a texture coordinate is `clamp(byte / 255, 0.01, 0.99)`, then repeat, linear filtering, one level:** the wrap of 328 stands, its bare 0 and 1 do not. `CODE`; that the game shows no line along the sky's faces and no ring round the sun is `GAME` (seen on an NVIDIA card, 3D-card renderer, 1024 × 768). Corrects 328. **The wrap:** `glTexParameterf` is called in three places and nowhere else: the capability probe (`0x422124`), the per-texture upload (`0x423732`) and the run-time texture (`0x413E9C`); all three set `GL_TEXTURE_WRAP_S` and `_T` to `GL_REPEAT` and both filters to `GL_LINEAR`. No `glTexEnv`, no texture matrix; `glTexCoord2f` takes the two floats of the queued record as they are. The upload loop runs once (the mipmap switch `0x4B1CC8` is 0 on every profile): one level, border 0, no repeated border is uploaded. **The profile** (`0x42216E`…`0x4222EB`) is chosen by `strstr`, case kept, on `GL_VENDOR` and `GL_RENDERER`, a later hit replacing an earlier one:<br>`3Dfx` → 0<br>`NEC` → 2<br>`RENDITION` → 1<br>`ATI` (vendor) or `RAGE PRO` (renderer) → 5<br>`NVIDIA` (vendor) or `RIVA` (renderer) → 4<br>`Real3D` (vendor) → 7<br>`Direct3D` (renderer) → 6<br>nothing found → 9, which runs the same code as 3 and 7 (`0x42277B`)<br>the two software renderers → 8.<br>The override `0x4AC010` is the constant 9 and nothing writes it (a developer's switch). A current GeForce says "NVIDIA Corporation": profile 4, whose own loop clamps the tables to [0, 1], that is nothing; an AMD card says "ATI Technologies Inc.": profile 5; an Intel one gets the default. **The clamp 328 missed** comes after the profile's own code, at `0x4228F4`: unless the profile is 8, a last loop over both UV tables turns every entry above 0.99 into 0.99 and every entry below 0.01 into 0.01 (the constants at `0x45CA24` and `0x45CA28`). So on every OpenGL card bytes 0, 1, 2 give 0.01 and bytes 253, 254, 255 give 0.99; the faces without UV bytes and the sprites, which take entries 0 and 255, span 0.01 … 0.99 of their texture. `ATI` / `RAGE PRO` keep their tighter [4.5 / 255, 250.5 / 255] on both axes; `NEC`, `RENDITION` and `Direct3D` have that on V and [0.01, 0.99] on U. **What it does to the picture:** with linear filtering and repeat the opposite edge is mixed in only within half a texel of the border; a clamped coordinate is 0.01 · N texels from it (N the texture's side), so the mix at the border is max(0, 0.5 − 0.01 · N): nothing from 50 texels up (64, 128, 256: the skies, the sun, the walls), 18% on a side of 32, 34% on 16, 42% on 8, 46% on 4, falling to nothing within that same fraction of a texel. A face that point-samples a corner of a tiny texture (the clock of *Wabbit on the run! 2* (`L01B`), 16 × 16 texture 116) still gets a mix of the four corners, a little less than without the clamp: the corner (255, 0) gives (0, 33, 99) unclamped, (0, 22, 88) clamped, (0, 0, 66) by the software renderer's rule. What the code cannot say: whether a current driver honours `GL_REPEAT` given as a float; anisotropic filtering or antialiasing forced from the driver's panel change how the border is sampled and are outside the program. For a tool: the PC rule is this clamp on both axes, for models, terrain and sprites alike; 328's "half-texel line of the opposite edge at the border of sprites and whole-texture faces" holds only for sides under 50 texels and never at full strength (N41, which corrects N29). | A blind check on the whole disc, the bar written before the run: "with the clamp, under 10% of the textured faces still mix more than 10% of the opposite edge at a corner" (null: 51.8% without the clamp). **Missed: 100,613 of 323,157 = 31.1%.** By the texture's smaller side: 32 texels 72,718 faces, 16 texels 18,197, 8 texels 2,161, 4 texels 7,537. What was learnt: about a fifth of the disc's faces (22.5%) sit on 32-texel textures, where the corner still mixes 18%, over 0.18 of a texel: a fraction of a screen pixel at the sizes the game shows them. So the reading explains the large textures outright and the 32-texel ones by size; for sides of 16 and under (8.6% of the faces) it predicts a faint mix that nobody reports in the game: too small to see, or something unread. The test that can fail: a close lossless screenshot of the back of the clock of `L01B` against a render made with this rule; wrong if the game's clock is clean gold where the rule predicts a mix. Not run. |
| 342 | **The animation roles: the code gives a meaning to one role only, 5 (the pose of the types that have no steps);** every other role is a label of the designers, and the same label means the same kind of animation across the disc. `CODE` for role 5, `DATA` for the labels. Roles 4 (rig), 7 (model) and 8 (sprite frames) are kinds of resource, not animations. On the data: the steps of the disc name 287 roles; for every role (from the steps of every level file, opcodes `0x30` and `0x34`): the steps that play it, the play mode, how many are static, free, travelling, dangerous, deleted at the end or cloning, the states that hold them, frames and ticks. What the counts say of the commonest:<br>**131** (5,721 steps, 4,663 placed): the rest pose: played once and held in 90%, control bit `0x1` (never moved, 340) in 92%, the step of state 1 for most objects; also the waiting step of scripts and of the pause menu's pages; 872 of these steps carry a cloning rule (emitters and spawners wait in it)<br>**168** (392): the walk: looping 87%, forward speed set in 83%: the patrols of 317<br>**190** (119): the attack: played once 81%, 71% in the designers' state 199 (where the "within N of Bugs" rules lead), dangerous control bits in 43%<br>**401** (4): a second attack: all four in state 199, all four dangerous<br>**156** (227): a one-off reaction: played once 78%, 76% in state 164, a quarter deleted at its end<br>**201** (94): breaking or going away: templates 91%, played once 95%, deleted at its end 89% (the crates of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`))<br>**203** (67): a travelling loop in states 236 and 206 (looping 99%, forward speed in 61%): probably the chase or the flight, not looked at one by one (`HYPOTHESIS`)<br>**211** (35): a still scripted pose in states 404 and 2 (static 100%)<br>**118** (891): the object's own main animation (once 48%, loop 28%; state 129)<br>**135** (426): a second pose, held, state 144: what the pirate on the crates of `L03A` waits in, crouched<br>**178** (64): "up": that pirate standing.<br>The state numbers are labels too: 199 attack, 164 triggered, 3 hit and 4 dying (the states the hazard block sends a hit object to, 318), 2 start (339). Over the other 60 roles with at least 30 steps (10,981 steps) the dominant mode covers **84.9%** of a role's steps on average, against a pooled null of 43.5% (bars: 75%, and null + 15): **held**.
