| 345 | **The torch's glow is closed on a lossless screenshot of the PC game:** one layer is enough, the blue never falls, and the little extra blue at middle distance is the mode 1 texture conversion. `GAME` for the screenshot's numbers (the picture's resolution, the camera's position and which torch of `L03A` was shot were not recorded: the fit can be repeated on that picture, not remade from another; the picture is not published), `CODE` for the conversion; the picture rebuilt from the code missed its bar by little (third cell). Closes 305 and the open point of 338; the centring of 338 stays the thing a renderer has to do. **The screenshot** (the PC game on OpenGL, a lossless PNG): the sea is (0, 0, 63); along a horizontal line through the flame, at 20, 40, 60, 80 and 100 pixels from it: (84, 75, 94), (66, 57, 93), (34, 31, 83), (6, 6, 67), (0, 0, 63). The blue never falls under the sea's: the "91 → 80" read earlier on a speedrun video was the video's compression, as 338 supposed, and the `Direct3D`-profile hypothesis has nothing left to explain. **The extra blue at 40 and 60 pixels is neither the sky nor a second, colder layer: it is the mode 1 conversion.** `0x420D90` pushes every texel to full brightness by adding (255 − m) to each channel (m the largest of the three): a dim yellow texel of the glow's rim becomes nearly white, (255, 233, 213) and up, while the bright middle stays yellow, (255, 241, 154). Solving the samples for the colour blended in (red gives the alpha, the sea has none): its blue is 157, 179, 213, 233 going outwards: the target whitens as the glow fades, which is what lifts the blue at middle distance. For a tool: no second layer, no other colour: (255, 241, 154) with a normal blend is the game's. What is left of the torch is 338's list: centre the glow (bit `0x8000000` of the second dword of opcode `0x16`), draw sprites after the sorted faces, and 341's 0.01 … 0.99 for the sprite's texture (N45). | A check with the bar written before the run: one quad, texture 250 of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) converted for mode 1 with the gamma, bilinear, 0.01 … 0.99 of the texture (341), blended over (0, 0, 63), sampled along the quad's middle row, the half width fitted in 93 … 115 pixels; bar: largest channel error 8. **Missed by little: the largest error is 12** (red 78 against 66 and green 65 against 57 at 40 pixels); the blue is within 2 at all four distances (96, 94, 82, 66 against 94, 93, 83, 67). An observation made after the run, and reported as such (two numbers fitted against twelve): with the sampled row 10 to 13% of the quad's height off its middle and the half width 98 to 106, all twelve values come within 2 ((85, 77, 94), (67, 58, 93), (32, 29, 82), (6, 6, 67)) and the 100-pixel sample is pure sea. That offset is 51 to 67 units of the 512-unit quad: the measured line went through the flame, which rises from the point the glow is centred on (338), not through the glow's middle. |
| 346 | **Two objects carried to the camera (two sky shells in one level): the depth buffer decides between them, not their order in the list;** and the sky's cut-outs are blended after all the opaque faces, farthest first, without the alpha test. `CODE`; the survey by rays is `DATA`; the pictures it predicts have not been compared with the game, and the dotted pink band of *The Planet X File! 3* (`L05A5`) is a `HYPOTHESIS`: a prediction not seen in the game. Completes 267 and 306, answers the open point of 339 (the depth of a camera follower). **1. A camera follower has no drawing path of its own.** The scene loop `0x44C620` walks the drawn objects and gives every model part to the vertex callback (`0x4113E0` on OpenGL) and to `0x44CD00` → `0x411BA0`, which only queues the faces (306). What `0x44CD00` does for a backdrop, read whole: with bit `0x2000000` of `object+0xC` the far word is capped at the level's and two words are written, `0x4B3B74` and `0x4B3224`, which have writers only and no reader in the whole program (the PlayStation's separate ordering table for backdrops, dead on the PC); for type 9 or bit `0x20000000` the draw distance is the largest (65,535: no far rejection, no fade: the fade starts at 49,151 units and the domes are at most about 30,000 away). Nothing else: no depth function, no depth range, no clear, no flush of their own. `glDepthFunc(GL_LEQUAL)`, `glEnable(GL_DEPTH_TEST)` and `glDepthRange` once at start-up (`0x40DB58`…), no `glDepthMask` anywhere in the program, `glClear(colour \| depth)` once a frame at the top of the flush (`0x41256E`), and `glOrtho(…, 65536.0)` with the vertex's own view depth as z (`0x413A20`): the depth buffer is linear in the view depth over 0 … 65,536 units, the same for the sky and for the world. **2. The three answers.**<br>Order: faces are queued per texture as the objects are walked; the flush `0x412540` draws the untextured ones, then, per texture, in texture order, every opaque face of the frame, sky and world together. With `GL_LEQUAL` and depth writes on, the nearer opaque face wins at every pixel whatever was drawn first; only at exactly equal depth the later texture wins<br>Depth: read and written, always, by every face of every pass (opaque, sorted, sprites). Two shells carried to the camera are resolved per pixel by their distance from the camera, the word of opcode `0x1E` included: that word is what makes one shell nearer above the horizon and the other below it<br>Cut-outs: no alpha test on a modern card. A face whose texture has a transparent palette entry, and every semi-transparent face, waits; after all the opaque faces they go in one array with the mean view depth of their vertices, are sorted farthest first (`0x41353E`) and drawn one by one with blending, the alpha test disabled (`0x4135A0`: enabled only when `0x4AC030` is set, which is the `Direct3D` profile alone), depth test and depth writes on. A transparent texel changes no colour but does write its depth: no harm, because everything farther is already in the picture. It would cut only what is drawn later and farther: a sorted face out of order by its mean depth, or a blended sprite (drawn after the sorted faces, unsorted, 338) behind a cut-out: the game's own small flaw, not observed.<br>**3. The two levels with two shells** (rays from the camera on a grid of 5 degrees against the followers' models; no backface culling; a cut-out face counts as see-through everywhere). *"Witch" way to Albuquerque? 2* (`L02B2`): object 39 is not a plain dome: it has a low layer of clouds about 6,300 units above the camera, its own dome behind at 17,000 … 25,000, a ring of cut-outs 11,400 … 15,900 away between −7° and +23° (the trees) and a bowl about 10,000 below. The clone of template 131, raised 3,000 by the word of `0x1E`, is a dome 14,300 above the camera, 15,000 … 24,000 away in the upper sky, with opaque hills round the horizon. By depth: below −10° object 39; round the horizon 131's hills with 39's cut-out trees blended in front; from +6° to +70° 131's dome, nearer than 39's, with 39's cloud layer in front of it wherever that layer has a face; above +70° 39's clouds. Shown over the sphere: 39 71.6%, 131 45.4% (they overlap where the cut-outs are). Painted in list order, the last covering: 39 10.4%, 131 89.9%: a flat blue sky without clouds is that wrong picture. *"Witch" way to Albuquerque? 1* (`L02B1`): by depth object 47 shows over 65.8% and the clone of 143 over 50.2%; in list order 47 keeps 0.1%. **4. Inside one dome the same rule matters.** *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`), object 0: 325 opaque triangles 12,000 … 25,000 units away, 52 cut-out ones (textures 1, 2, 8: the islets with the palms, the ships) and 20 blended ones, in front of the dome's opaque faces along every ray but three. In the game they are blended after the whole dome is in the picture, farthest first, and nothing shows; a renderer that writes their depth before the opaque faces behind are drawn gets dark boxes of the clear colour behind every transparent texel: the cure is the game's order, not an alpha test. `L05A5`, object 23: 349 opaque triangles, 22 cut-out, 476 blended (238 quads, counted as triangles): 98 of mode 1 on texture 235 (32 × 32, a grey speckle) with pink and purple vertex colours ((99, 0, 60), (63, 29, 58)…), 115 and 25 of mode 0 on textures 244 and 245; they stand 7,600 … 18,000 units out, in front of the nearest opaque face along 425 of the 462 rays that meet them. By the code they are drawn after the dome, blended with the PC's one blend function (alpha from the mode 1 copy, m − m / 4: faint), the vertex colour multiplying the whitened texel: **a faint band of pink dots in front of the dome is what the PC game should show there: predicted, not seen in the game.** What the code cannot say: the precision of the driver's depth buffer (the shells of the disc are hundreds of units apart where they cross). For a tool: (1) draw all the followers together with the depth test and depth writes on: first every opaque face, then the cut-out and blended faces of all of them sorted farthest first by mean depth, blended, no alpha test; (2) the game shares one depth buffer between sky and world, the same picture as long as the world is nearer than the sky; a tool with a free camera may clear the depth before the world, and should say so; (3) keep the word of `0x1E`: it is what splits the sky between two shells. The 15 files with two followers or more (the files whose placed objects carry the camera bit of 339, with their blend modes) say what to expect level by level; in the *Era selector* (`LS01`) the five skies are cut by area, and its template 12, a sixth dome, is cloned by no rule (N46). | A blind check on the disc, the bar written before the run, the four levels above left out as seen: "by the depth rule at least 90% of the followers wider than 10,000 units that can be on screen together show over at least 5% of the sphere". **Missed: 13 of 16 = 81.2%**; the contrast, painted in list order: 9 of 16 = 56.2% (so the prediction had power). Looked at after the run: the three misses (*The Conquest for Planet X!* (`L05B1`) object 9, *Wabbit or Duck Season?* (`LB01`) object 70, the credits' object 8) are 14 to 20 blended triangles each, met on 0.4 … 0.7% of the sphere and shown wherever they are met: a planet, a sun, not shells; the premise on their size was poor, and the bar stays missed. What the run does show: in *The Carrot-henge Mystery 3* (`L02C3`) the clones of templates 91 and 132 split the sky 82.5% / 17.5% by depth and 0% / 100% in list order; in `L05B1` the blended objects 8 and 9 come before the dome 10 in the list: in list order they vanish, by depth they show (24.0%, 0.7%). In the game, not run: in `L02B2`, looking up from about (6854, −3360, 20011): clouds overhead, a blue dome with clouds in front from a little above the horizon to +70°, hills and fields round the horizon with trees in front; wrong if the sky is flat blue without clouds. In `L05A5`: a faint band of pink and purple dots all round the sky (a lossless screenshot is enough); wrong if the sky has no dots at all. |
| 347 | **The type 8 record with flag `0xB` hides the part until the next TRS record that names it:** 280 read in the code; and the game draws rig parts, not meshes, which is why the knight keeps his helmet. `CODE` for the record, `DATA` for the rigs and the streams; in the game the knight is remembered always with his helmet on, the visor lifting and dropping (`HYPOTHESIS`, reported by players, not a fresh test). Of the readings that were open — (a) hidden until a TRS, (b) back to the rest pose, (c) left where the animation before put it — it is (a). **1. The record**, in the frame applier `0x44E5A0`, case `0x80000` (`0x44E86E`). Its first dword: part id in bits 0–15, type in 16–19, **flag nibble in 20–23**, length in words in 24–31. The part is looked up by id in the object's chain (`object+0x78`, id at `part+0x18`).<br>Flag bit 1 clear (nibbles 0, 2, 4, 6, 8, `0xA`), by `nibble >> 1` through the table at `0x44EA90`: 0 creates the part (`0x448B60`: a free record of the pool `0x4B2240`, `0xB8` bytes each, flag word `part+8` = `0x8008`: no model, not shown); 1 to 4 create it with flag word `0x40`, `0x80`, `0x100`, `0x200`; 5 (nibble `0xA`) creates nothing and does `part+8 &= 0x7FFF`: show<br>Flag bit 1 set: if the other three bits are `0xA`, that is nibble `0xB` (`0x44E8A2`): the three dwords of the part's scale are zeroed, five words of its local matrix are zeroed, and `part+9 \|= 0x80`, that is bit `0x8000` of the flag word. Any other odd nibble deletes the part (`0x448C10`).<br>On the disc: flag 0 in every rig (14,492 of 14,492), `0xB` in every animation (27,965 of 27,965): only "create" and "hide" are used; "show" and "delete" never. **2. What undoes it.** The TRS case (`0x44E6CA`) begins with `part+8 &= 0x7FFF`, whatever its flags; then flag 2 writes the rotation, flag 4 the scale, and the local matrix is rebuilt, flag 8 the translation (each skipped when the part has bit `0x400`, set by the simple movers' handler `0x43F690` on the parts it turns itself). A model link (type 2, `0x44E7AA`) rewrites the whole flag word (4 with a model part, 8 without): that is how a part created by the rig becomes drawable. **3. Who reads bit `0x8000`:** the renderer's front end `0x40A250`, called by the game loop before the scene loop. For every part with a model the chain composer `0x40A0B0` multiplies the local matrices up the parents, once a frame, and returns 0 when the composed 3 × 3 is all zero; then (`0x40A350`): composed all zero, or `part+9 & 0x80`: the visible byte `part+0xB4` = 0; else the matrix goes to `part+0x94` and the byte = 1. The scene loop (`0x44CB3E`) and the part drawer (`0x44CD8A`) draw a part only with that byte set; the shadows skip it too (315). For sprite parts the scene loop tests the bit itself (`0x44CBBD`). Nothing else reads it. **So (a):** not drawn, no shadow, from that frame until the next TRS record that names the part. Not (b): nothing is taken from the rig or from a rest pose; a rig has no TRS records at all (the knight's: 29 creates, 28 parents, 26 model links). Not (c) for the part named; but (c) is what happens to a part an animation does not name: the parts belong to the object and outlive the animation; a new animation changes only the parts its records name, the others keep their last pose and their hidden bit. That is why the streams open with a block that hides everything they do not use. A detail: the record zeroes the scale, so a TRS that brings the part back must carry the scale again, or the part comes back as a point, and its children with it. **4. The knight of *What's cookin', Doc? 6* (`L02A6`, model 112): the helmet is in the rig twice.** Meshes 5 and 6 (the helmet and the visor) are linked by rig parts 7 and 8 (on the head: parents 6 ← 5 ← 4 ← 3) and again by parts 25 and 26, children of part 24 with 27 and 28: a second, loose helmet. In the walk (role 168) and in roles 204, 2, 174, 190, 208 the first block hides parts 24–28 and parts 7 and 8 are driven all along (role 168: both in 15 of 16 blocks; role 204: the visor, part 8, in 37 of 41 blocks: it lifts and drops): the knight walks with his helmet on. Roles 182, 183, 249, 164 are the other way round: the body (parts 3–23) hidden in block 0, only the loose helmet driven; role 206 (state 3: he is hit) hides the head's helmet in block 0 and the rest of the body in blocks 1 to 6 while 25 and 26 are driven: he falls into his helmet; roles 199 and 348 start from the loose helmet, bring the body back part by part and hide the loose one: he gets up again. The scene loop walks the object's chain and draws each part's own model pointer (`part+0x14`), so one mesh can be on screen through either part, or twice. A tool that keeps one transform per mesh lets the part linked last win, here 25 and 26, the hidden ones, and the helmet goes: keep one transform per rig part, and draw a mesh once for each shown part that links it. On the disc only the knight's rig links a mesh more than once: 4 rigs of 2,426, one per file (*What's cookin', Doc? 1* (`L02A1`), `L02A6`, *"Witch" way to Albuquerque? 2* (`L02B2`), *The Carrot-henge Mystery 4* (`L02C4`)); 46 (stream, mesh) pairs lose a shown mesh that way. **5. The time machine of the *Era selector* (`LS01`, object 17 and its copies, model 174):** parts 10 and 11 (meshes 7 and 8, the light beams) are hidden in the resting role 131 and in roles 250, 251, 1; roles 154 and 199 drive them: the beams exist only while the machine works. One part per mesh: (a) as it is. **6. Who sets bit 4 of level byte 111 in `L02A6`** (`DATA`): zones 7 and 20. Trigger 145 copies it to bit 8 and clears both every tick, so bit 8 means "Bugs is in zone 7 or 20 now": outside them the knight is template 147 (a stand-in going between objects 53 and 54), inside them 147 clones 148 (the one that walks, attacks and is hit) and deletes itself; 148 goes back to 147 when the bit falls. What this means for the counts of 280: the 4,941 model–animation pairs that hide a part for good and the 596 models with parts missing in the pose shown are, but for the knight, what the game does: parts hidden on purpose (a second club, a hole's cover, beams, effects that start invisible) (N47). | Two blind checks on the disc, the bars written before the run; left out as seen: the five levels 280 names, `L02A6`, `LS01`, the `_8` files. P1: the record zeroes the scale, so the first TRS for a part after a `0xB` record, in the same stream, carries the scale (flag 4): **held, 2,371 of 2,371** (bar 99%; null, every other TRS record: 147,007 of 1,732,572 = 8.48%, bar under 20%). P2, a survey with one bar, about how the models were made: when a stream leaves hidden a part of a mesh linked by more than one rig part, another part of that mesh is shown at the end of the stream: **held, 56 of 56** (bar 80%) on the files not looked at before the run; 82 of 82 when the seven files left out as already seen (the five levels 280 names, `L02A6`, `LS01`) are counted too; all the pairs belong to the knight's rig. In the game, not run: the knights of `L02A6` walk with the helmet on, the visor lifting and dropping; when one is hit the body goes and the helmet alone is left for a moment, then he gets up; a time machine at rest in `LS01` has no light beams; wrong if a walking knight is bare-headed or a resting machine has its beams. |
| 348 | **Save byte 0 is not "lives": the code only ever writes 3 to it and never reads it, and the two levels that test it test a constant.** `CODE` for what the two executables do with the byte, `DATA` for the rules of the disc; that the game has no lives and no game over is `HYPOTHESIS`, reported by players, not measured here (what the code shows is that nothing reads the byte); "a leftover of a lives system" is a reading, not a fact (`HYPOTHESIS`). Corrects the label of 335. **Where the name came from: the documents, not the game.** O102 named index 1 "lives" and index 2 "their maximum": they are the health and its maximum (six half carrots); it left index 0 (= 3 at startup) unnamed. An early reading said in passing "byte 0 is 3: the lives": a guess from the number 3, with nothing read behind it, repeated in later counts. **What `bugs.exe` does with `config+0x10040`** (one byte): four direct accesses, all of them "write 3":<br>`0x406400`, in the startup (`0x4061B0`; O102's function, `0x406190` in the other build): the 256 save bytes zeroed, bytes 1 and 2 = 6, byte `0x10` = the language, byte 0 = 3<br>`0x40734C` and `0x407430`, in the save service `0x4071B0`, next to health and its maximum = 6: an empty slot, a new game<br>`0x406E8B`, in the game loop: the branch taken when the level-change word `bugs.exe+0xB39BC` is 2 (`0x406E2B`; 1 is a level change): pause flags cleared, health = 6, byte 0 = 3, the loop returns. No instruction of the program writes 2 into that word (it gets 0, or 1): the branch is dead. It reads like "start again with full health and 3 of whatever byte 0 counted": if the game ever had lives, this is where they were given back; nothing takes one away any more.<br>No instruction reads index 0 by itself, none adds to it or subtracts from it. Action `0x30` (`0x42D1A0`), which O102 quotes as "if (lives != max) lives++", is health: `if (byte 1 != byte 2) byte 1 += 1; else the byte the rule names += 1`. Every other access to the table goes through a rule's index. **The PlayStation executable does the same:** one direct access, the startup at `0x80015EF0` (table zeroed, health and maximum 6, byte 0 = 3); every other access to the table is indexed. Apply mends what is on the OpenGL side and touches no file; a remount of the disc mends nothing by itself (nothing is read until the next level load); a level load mends everything. The words to note when it happens: `bugs.exe+0xB1C8C` (names uploaded), `+0xB1CA4` (the scratch buffer, 0 = every upload skipped), `+0xB1CB8` / `+0xB1CD4` (the level's texture section), `+0xAC094` (the renderer), `+0x5CA83C` (the fog). | None on the disc: a reading of the code. | 354 | **The detach zip: when Bugs leaves a parent the game sweeps from the parent's ORIGIN to where he stands and puts him where the sweep stops;** for a vector within a few units of an axis line the sweep's crossing arithmetic overflows 32 bits and the PC's x87 square root makes the far point win, so Bugs lands at the first 320-unit cell line next to the origin (the recorded zip reproduced to the unit by the game's own routines, run on the level's data); by the code the PlayStation's square root avoids it; the local offset is written afresh at every attach. `CODE` for the mechanism (N56, N58: the detach `0x43E11E`…`0x43E28B`, the jump-off detach `0x43C06B`…`0x43C2C3`, the standing test `0x4343A0`, the attach `0x43D0D9`…`0x43D2ED`, the sweep's slopes `0x43504C`, its sub-cell stepping `0x435441`… and its cell crossing `0x43569B`…`0x43571C`, the root `0x4079E0`; the PlayStation twins `0x8002A5CC` (the detach), `0x80019474` (the sweep), `0x8004F72C` (`SquareRoot0`)). **`CODE + GAME` for the zip itself**: a recording on the drawbridge of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A` object 127, pivot (6500, −1200, 11000), yaw word 3071) shows Bugs put at **(6439, 11000)** on walking off its far end, and the game's own sweep, run on the level's collision blocks with the recorded vector, gives **(−61, 0) from the pivot: (6439, 11000), to the unit**. The PlayStation part is `CODE` only. **What the detach does.** While Bugs stands on a parent (`+0x70`, his flag `+0x14 & 0x10`) his world position is composed every tick from his local offset `+0x1A4` / `+0x1A8` / `+0x1AC` through the parent's matrix; the offset is written at the attach (`0x43D1EE`: local = R⁻¹ × (Bugs − parent) plus the tick's move; `+0x1A8` = the parent's box top + 4) and is never cleared at the detach (a log keeps its last value after the parent goes to 0). The standing test `0x4343A0` (the five points of Bugs's box, ±70 × ±40 turned by his heading, against the parent's box of the moment, all differences in 16 bits) runs every tick; **when it fails, the detach `0x43E173`**: offset = Bugs − parent origin (X and Z, 32 bits), start = the parent's origin at Bugs's Y, **the wall sweep `0x434E40` on that vector**, Bugs = parent origin + the swept offset, links cleared, flags `& ~0x214 \| 2`. With a free sweep he does not move (walking off the end onto the dock: three normal detaches in the same recording); with a cut sweep he is pulled back along the line toward the origin: **that is the zip, always toward the parent's origin, never elsewhere**. The same sweep from the origin is done by the jump-off path (`0x43C1A4`…: a jump off the end zips as much as a walk off it) and by a type 14 object leaving its own parent (`0x442DC3`…); the other releases (the pole, the climb, a parent letting go with effect `0x4000`, the vehicle's `0x20000000`) do not sweep. **Why the sweep cuts.** At its start the sweep computes the two 12-bit slopes and the two points where the ray meets the next 320-unit cell line in z and in x; at every cell crossing (`0x43569B`) it chooses the nearer by the squares of their distances from the start in **32-bit `imul`**, then square roots. For a vector nearly parallel to X the z-line point lies `\|dx\| × Δz / \|dz\|` units away and **its squared distance overflows above 46,340**; when the wrapped square is negative the PC's root (`fild; fsqrt; fistp`) gives the x87 "integer indefinite", −2,147,483,648, **so the far point is always judged the nearer**, `0x436CD0` finds no block there, and the sweep stops at the last accepted sample: **the sub-cell before the first 320-unit line along the main axis** (the line + 39 going −, the line − 40 going +; 6439 = 6400 + 39). With a still steeper ratio (over about 1,158) the stop is the start itself: Bugs lands on the pivot. The recording: the local offset (9, 0, 1558) through yaw 3071 (269.9°) is the world vector (−1558, +6.6): cut at 61. The band for this pivot: local x 2, 3, 5, 6 (4 is free) and −3…−4 cut at 61, ±1 land on the pivot, 7 and more, −1, −2, −5 are free. And independently of the overflow, **any real wall, `0x7F` cell or block side between the origin and Bugs at his height pulls him back to it** (object 129 of the same level, the bridge along +X, has one 1420 from its pivot). **The PlayStation.** The detach and the sweep are the same code line by line (the 320 and 40 cells, `sll 12; div` slopes, the squares by `mult` + `mflo` with the same wrap); **the one line that differs is the root**: `SquareRoot0` uses the GTE's leading-zero count and a table, and for the same negative bits returns 31,968 (large), so the near point wins and the sweep goes on. By the code the bridge zip is a PC bug of the x87 conversion and does not exist on the PlayStation for this bridge; a wrap landing on a small positive would fool both, but none of this bridge's vectors does. **The offset at every attach (N58).** The attach writes the three words anew from the new parent's frame; a Bugs already on a parent (flag `0x10`) cannot be taken by a second box; the vehicle's take and the pole grab do not go through the attach and never use `+0x1A4` for the composition; the detach reads Bugs's composed position, never the offset: **no state of the offset can point the zip elsewhere than the parent's origin**. Chaining two bridges gives two zips, each toward its own pivot. The only other knob is a moving parent: the jump-off detach runs before this tick's carry, so on a platform moving across the axis the cross component is one tick of its motion off. **Where it can happen:** 271 standable objects of the disc have a box that reaches over 320 from their origin (the five drawbridges of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1*, the conveyors of *The Carrot Factory 1* (`L04C1`) with a reach of 5760, *The Planet X File! 3* (`L05A5`) object 29, *Nowhere* (`MERLIN`) object 29, *The Planet X File! 1* (`L05A1`) object 55); the game's own routines give the landing for any (origin, exit point) before anyone goes there (N62). | The check that held: the game's own sweep, run on the recorded tick (start at the pivot's origin, vector (−1558, +6.6) at Bugs's height, on the level's collision blocks), had to land where the recording put Bugs: **(6439, 11000), to the unit**; the three normal detaches of the same recording had to come out free, and did. In the game, not run: walk off the +z end of the same bridge with the local x (`player+0x1A4`) at 2, 3, 5 or 6: Bugs must land at x 6439; at ±1 on the pivot (6500); at 7 or more nothing must happen; on the PlayStation the same walk-offs must never zip. Wrong if a zip lands anywhere but on the line toward the pivot, or if the PlayStation zips on this bridge. |

The wall height of 640 units drawn by the BBLIT Viewer was its own drawing constant, not a fact of the game. **`CODE` and checked with the game's own routines for the distance** (N67: the sweep's dispatch `0x434F8F`…`0x434F99`, the X-only copy `0x435BA8`…`0x436172` with its loop `0x435C5C`…`0x435FA2`, the Z-only copy `0x436363`…; the game's own sweep run on the three levels: the recorded stops 6679 and 8440 to the unit, and 6720 for C, below). **The sweep tests a point.** The wall sweep `0x434E40` takes a position, a move and the query record (298); nothing of Bugs's box (±70 × ±40, turned by his heading) or of his contact radius enters it: the five points of the box are for the object boxes (322) and for the standing test on a parent (354). So "how far from the wall Bugs stops" is "how far from the wall's line his origin parks", and his body overlaps the wall by whatever the box sticks out (up to 70 ahead, 40 to the side). **Three copies of the sweep, chosen by the move** (`0x434F8F`): `dx == 0` → the Z-only copy, `dz == 0` → the X-only copy, both non-zero → the two-axis copy (the one of 354 and 355). **They do not sample the same points.** The one-axis copy's loop takes the next sub-cell line along the axis as its sample (the line going +, the line − 1 going −: the first coordinate inside the next sub-cell), finds the block, tests the entered sub-cell (`0x7F` unless the first sample, a step of more than 100 above the mover) **and only then asks whether the move had already ended** (`0x435F76`…): a move that ends inside a sub-cell is followed by one more sample, the line beyond; when that sub-cell is a wall the move is cut back to the last accepted line (`0x435FA7`…). Hence, by the routines: **a walk toward a `0x7F` line along an axis parks the origin at the line − 40 going + and the line + 39 going −, whatever the speed** (1, 5, 12, 27, 30, 70 a tick: all the same), and from that place every further move along the axis is cut to 0 (the lookahead is the wall): `L03A` from 6700 with −21 → 6679, with −25 → 6679; `L02A2` → 8440; `L01B` from 6700 with +20 → 6720, +21 → 6720. A Bugs put by hand inside the last sub-cell (6650) and moved −1 stays where he is: not pulled back, simply unable to move along that axis. **The two-axis copy samples the crossings before the endpoint and nothing beyond it** (355): with any cross component the origin walks into the last free sub-cell: `L03A` (−25, 1) → 6675, (−59, 1) → 6641, **(−60, 1) → 6640, the wall's line itself** (the point x = 6640 is the first integer of the free sub-cell; the crossing sample for the line going − is 6639, inside the wall), (−61, 1) → cut back to 6679; going +, `L01B` (1, 15) from 6744 → 6759, (1, 16) → cut to 0. **The origin's nearest place to a `0x7F` line: the line itself going −, the line − 1 going +, never inside the wall's sub-cell.** Tick after tick at a heading a few degrees off the axis the origin creeps to 1…2 units from the line and the handler's slide lets him walk along the wall. **The recorded C, read with this.** The straight walk +z of C parks at 6720 by the routines, like A and B; the log has 6720 for 0.2 s, then 6732, then 6744: two single advances of one air step (12), each on a row where the position had just been rewritten from outside by the Y hold of the recording (x and y jump in the same row, so the tick's move had an x component): **from (3220, 6720) the routines give (0, 12) → 0 and (29, 12) → (29, 12), landing 6732; from 6732 again 6744; from 6744, (0, 20) → 0.** So the "16…20 along Z against 40 along X" is not a rule of the axis: it is the two-axis copy on ticks whose move was not pure. The whole of C was walked with series 14 (falling) under the Y hold; the `0x7F` test does not depend on Y, so the parking is the same in the air. **Where the origin does enter a `0x7F` sub-cell:** not by the sweep: by the holes of 354 (the detach from a parent's origin), 356 (the per-axis re-sweeps after a box correction) and 357 (the long box's sector), by a position written from outside, and by the crate's carry of 319 (Bugs += done − asked, unswept); the handler then puts him back to the saved position when he ends a tick in a `0x7F` sub-cell (309). | The test run, in the game: at each of three walls, with Bugs's Y held by hand at 5 and 1 above the top of the block and at the top and 5 below it, the wall had to stop him in the first two and let him through in the last two; **three of three held**, and the stops were at 6679, 8440 and 6720. The check that held on the code: the game's own sweep run with the recorded moves had to give the three recorded stops to the unit; it did, and the 6732 and 6744 of C came out only with the x component the log shows. Not run, clean: walk +z at the wall of `L01B` with no hold on Y: the origin must park at 6720 and never advance; approach an axis wall at 1° off the axis: the origin must creep to within 1…2 of the line and slide along it. Wrong if a walk along an axis ever parks closer than 39 or farther than 40 from the line, or if the origin ever ends a tick inside a `0x7F` sub-cell without a hole of 354…357 or an outside write. |
| 359 | **The PC's save slots: level byte 10 is the request to the save service (4 new game, 5 load, 6 save),** the slot is save byte 159 kept in 1…6 by the title's slot page, `Savegame%d.dat` holds the 320-byte save block (LevID, checksum, pad words and the 256 save bytes) and `Savedata.dat` is the index of the slots (a mask and a summary per slot) that the load checks first, so the two files go together; a `Savegame0.dat` is a save made while byte 159 was 0, which no menu loads back; and the two Windows folders players know (Program Files as administrator, VirtualStore otherwise) are Windows's redirection of the game's relative path. `CODE` (N63: `save_service` `0x4071B0` whole, the save `0x407510`, the boot reader of the index `0x407710`, the title's objects 33 and 34 of `title.bze`, the actions `0x5D` and `0x64`, the strings at `0x45F3B8`); the two folders are a fact of Windows known to players (`GAME` by their use), not of the game. **The request.** The service reads **level byte 10** (`0x4B226A`) once a frame: **4 = new game**, **5 = load**, **6 = save**, and clears it; its result goes to level byte 8 after a save (`0x0A` written, `0x14` not written); a load and a new game set level bytes 27 = `0x10`, 28 = 0, 37 = 1 at their start (27 = 4 when the file cannot be read). Who sets it: the title's slot page (object 34: level byte 10 = 5 for "Load", = 4 for "New game") and the save points of the levels (type 16 objects with the rule "level byte 10 = 6 when level byte 18 == 1": the *Era selector* (`LS01`) 184 and 185, *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`) 263 and 264 and *2* 269 and 270, *"Witch" way to Albuquerque? 1* (`L02B1`) and *2*, and more). **The slot is save byte 159** (`config+0x100DF`): the slot page moves it with the pad (left −1, right +1, up −3, down +3, clamped to 1…6; action `0x64` skips the empty slots on the way), so **the menu never leaves 1…6**; object 33 (the first page) sets it to 0 and then to 1 on its own rules; a new game and a load keep it. **The load** (`0x4071F9`): the slot's bit must be set in **the mask `0x4B28E4`** (bit slot − 1; with the byte at 0 the shift is by 31: bit 31, never set: **slot 0 cannot be loaded**), else nothing happens; then `..\bin\Savegame%d.dat` (320 bytes) is read over the block `config+0x10000`…`+0x1013F` (the LevID, the checksum, the pad words, the 256 save bytes from +0x40) with save byte 16 kept (the language) and save byte 54 = 0; when the file cannot be read, the new-game defaults (save byte 0 = 3, 1 and 2 = 6, 14 = `0x38`…); with the file read, save byte 14 = the low byte of the block's LevID when it is not 0, else `0x38`. **The save** (`0x407510`): the checksum of the 320 bytes (`0x42E750`) into the dword at +4; `Savegame%d.dat` written whole; then **the index**: the slot's bit set in `0x4B28E4`, the slot's summary written into the block (slots 1…6 only: `0x4B28E8 + 0x2C × (slot − 1)`: word 0 = save byte 252 << 8 \| save byte 7 (the golden carrots), word 1 = save byte 5 (the clocks)), and **the whole block `0x4B28E0`, 360 bytes, written to `..\bin\Savedata.dat`**; if that write fails the bit is cleared again. At boot `0x407710` reads it (or creates an empty one), puts the mask's low byte into **level byte 4** (the title's rules: no saves → no load page) and copies the six slots' summary bytes into level bytes 43…54 and 59…64 (what the load page shows through action `0x5D`). **This is why a save cannot be replaced by copying `SavegameN.dat` alone: the load refuses a slot whose bit is not in `Savedata.dat`, and the page shows the old summary.** **`Savegame0.dat`** is written by any save that happens while save byte 159 is 0: the service writes the file (the `%d` is the byte as it is), sets bit 31 of the mask and no summary, **and no menu can load it back**. Byte 159 is 0 in a fresh `config.pc` and after object 33's rule; a level entered without the slot page (the level select of the cheats, 336; a warp) and saved there writes slot 0. **The two folders:** the game opens the files by the relative path `..\bin\` from its working folder; started as administrator that is the game's own `bin` under Program Files; without administrator rights Windows redirects the writes to the same path under the VirtualStore folder of the local application data: two independent sets of six slots, twelve in all, each with its own `Savedata.dat`, chosen by the rights the game runs with, not by the game. | None on the disc beyond the rules of `title.bze` and the save points (listed above). Wrong if a slot loads without its bit, if a save touches only one file, or if the menu ever shows a slot outside 1…6. |

That is the whole of the "sound glitch" in the code: a value under 50 is a mute for new tracks, not for the running one. No rule of the disc uses `0x7A`…`0x7F`: the volume pages are driven by the code. **Sound FX** = `config.pc +0x30` (`bugs.exe+0x5CA850`): actions `0x7C` (+10 up to 100, then 0) and `0x7D` (−10 down to 0, then 100); the mixer `0x41EE30` multiplies every voice by the value and divides by 100: 0 is silence, no threshold. **Mono / Stereo: nothing.** No action touches a channel count; no field of the settings block is it: `+0x38` is 1 when a joystick was found, `+0x3C`…`+0x55` are the key and button bindings; the strings "Stereo" / "Mono" are nowhere in the executable (ASCII or UTF-16) nor in the game texts (the menus are pictures). Finding 324 had type 18 as "a see-saw that tilts under Bugs and eases back": this is the rule itself. **How a see-saw works (type 18, mode `0x001`).** Every tick, for the object (`esi`): S = its word `+0x1E` (opcode `0x1E`; here 32); **only when the player is its child and it is the player's parent** (`0x43F9A6`…`0x43F9B9`: the pointer `0x4B324C`, nothing else: a weight or any other object never tilts it): `edx` = Bugs's local x on it (`player+0x1A4`, the attach's offset, 354); then: **local x ≥ 0** (Bugs between the origin and the far end): unless bit 2 of the low byte of `+0x17C` (the word of opcode `0x1D`), **the tilt word `+0xE0` (the rotation about the plank's own z) += S × x / (528 + R) a tick, up to +356** (`0x43FA3E`: `0x164` = 31.3° of 4096), and Bugs's local Y on the plank (his transform block `+0xC0`, word `+0x18`) += S × tilt / (528 + R) each tick (he follows the dip); **local x < 0** (behind the origin, inside the animation's −50): unless bit 1 of that byte, the same toward −356; **here the word is 1: bit 1 set, the back side locked**, bit 2 clear: the front side tilts; **Bugs off it: tilt −= tilt / 16 a tick** (`0x43FA91`, `0x43FAAD`): it eases back to level in some forty ticks. So it is not a balance on a middle pivot: **a plank hinged at its origin, whose far end dips under Bugs by 528 × sin(31.3°) = 274 at most** (his own Y on it follows), and which is level whenever he is not on it. | 366 | **How a rule fires and what a clone's life is, as one mechanism: the type 14 rule walk judges the current step's rules in order,** and a rule that fires without effect `0x8000` (go on) or `0x10` (stay) sends the object to the state its "next" word names, so it fires once; a trigger (type 16) walks its whole list every tick and clones on every tick its condition holds unless a byte or a stop rule guards it; a clone born in a tick runs its first tick in that same tick; the engine keeps no memory of a clone. `CODE` (the type 14 walk `0x44313B`…`0x444323`, the type 16 handler `0x444750`…`0x444CBF`, the loader's resolver `0x42E770`, the run loop `0x44887A`…`0x4488F7`, the cloning routine `0x448EB0`); `DATA` for the counts on the disc. Completes 311 and 314. (N73.) **The nodes.** A rule (opcode `0x31`) is a node of 0x24 bytes: `+4` its key (the step it belongs to), `+6` "next" (the state it leads to), `+8` and `+0xC` masks on the object's two flag words, `+0x10` the eight rule bytes (331), `+0x18` the effect, `+0x1C` p24, `+0x20` p28. After an object is read, the resolver turns the state numbers into list offsets (a state's slots → step offsets; a step's rule key → its first rule; a rule's next → that state); **a next of 0 stays 0, which is the first state stored in the object** (state 1 in 32 of the 33 rules of the disc that reach it). **The walk, each tick, in this order** (the step's rules are consecutive; the walk runs while the key is the step's): the two masks; the gates in the effect word (`0x40` the animation's frame == p24 at a new frame; **`0x4` the step's animation has reached its end this tick**, read from the animation state's flags `object+0x15C & 0x2000`, set by the player on the last frame; `0x2` / `0x20000` a random draw against p24 / p28); the condition byte; `0x2000` the id of what hit it == p28; the target (`0x8000000` Bugs, else the first live object of id p28 not already dying; `0x400000` sends it to state p24; `0x18000000` the distance to it; `0x40000000` its frame); **the action**; then the effects in this order: `0x8` detach, sounds, **the clone `0x100` / `0x40000`** (the template p28 at the marker's world point when the rule's frame has a marker, else at the object's position; **`0x80` hangs it from the marker part, else from the spawner's root, with `0x1000` from Bugs's root**; `0x40000` without `0x80` a random heading), `0x20000000` the same hanging for an existing object, **`0x1000` alone: the object itself on Bugs's root at zero offset** (304), `0x20` the side test, `0x4000000` delete by id, `0x80000` level change, `0x80000000` Bugs's series, `0x100000` restart point, `0x800` Bugs put at the object, `0x4000` let go, **`0x10000` the object dies (p28 == 1: Bugs is killed instead)**, `0x2000000` dies; **then `0x8000` → the next rule; else `0x10` → the walk ends and the object stays in its step; else the state change** (the state "next", its first slot's step, the animation restarted unless the new step has flag `0x40` and the same animation, the hit flags cleared) and the walk ends. After the walk the step's animation plays; at its end a step with control bit 4 kills the object, else the state's next slot, and after the last slot state 1 (314). **So:** a rule that changes the state fires once; a rule with `0x8000` or `0x10` fires on every tick its condition holds (turns, counters, emitters gated by `0x40` at a frame or `0x4` at the end); effect `0x10` is a "stop here" (2,917 rules on the disc); a rule with next ≠ 0 and `0x8000` never applies its next (81 on the disc). **A trigger** has no steps: its whole list every tick; the random gates, the condition — **when the condition is false the rule's next word is a skip label**: the walk jumps to the first rule with that key; when true: the target, the action, sounds, **the clone at the trigger's own position and heading** (`0x1000`: on Bugs's root; `0x40000` a random heading), delete by id, `0x800`, series, level change, then **`0x10000` the trigger dies, `0x8000` the next rule, else the walk ends**. A trigger's clone rule without `0x8000` therefore clones **every tick** its condition holds: the levels guard it with a byte the same rule's action sets, or with a stop rule before it. The 2000-unit test of a trigger with `+0xC & 4` only decides whether it registers itself in a global the camera reads (`0x4B3950`): its rules run either way. **The order, and the tick a clone is born in.** The run loop follows the chain the visibility pass builds from the live list in file order (the manager first: the object the loader itself makes, with its 47 children, 308), reads the next link after each handler and deletes a dead object right after its own turn; the cloning routine appends the clone to the tail: **a clone born in tick N runs its first tick in tick N**, after every object before the tail, and a first-step rule that is true fires then (a clone of a clone, the same). An object the camera has left out of the chain (culling without bit `0x40`, 294) does not run until the camera reaches it. **Nothing remembers a clone**: no count on the template, no search for a twin; the rule fires again whenever its object is back in that step. (N78.) **How they are chosen.** After the level's file name is taken from the table (and the localised `NAME_<digit>.BZE` has been tried and dropped, 344), **`level_load` reads the renderer in use, `bugs.exe+0xAC094` (0 the 8-bit software renderer, 1 the 24-bit one, 2 OpenGL), and when it is 0 compares the LevID with 7, 8, `0x15`, `0x16`, `0x17`, `0x18`, `0x19`, `0x22`, `0x36`: on a match the name becomes `NAME_8.BZE`** (`0x42EB11`…`0x42EB52`). What's up, Dock? 2* (`L03A2`), 24 *When Sam met Bunny* (`L03B`), 25 `L03C`, 34 *The Greatest Escape 3* (`L04B3`), 54 *La Corrida* (`LB04`)**. The word is the renderer *in use*, written by the window builder from `config.pc +4` with its silent fallbacks (352): **with the graphics set to the 8-bit software renderer, or after a fallback to it, every entry into one of those nine loads the `_8` file**: the swap sits inside `level_load`, which every way into a level passes through (a rabbit hole, an exit, a level change by a rule or a zone, the cheats' level select, a warp by memory, a restart); the `_8` file is loaded under the twin's LevID (344), so the rules, exits and save bytes know only the twin. **What each `_8` file changes** (objects / matched but different / terrain / textures with the same bytes in both): `L01D1`: 277 / 276, **object 26, the golden carrot at (25910, −1800, 8190), is not in the `_8`** (344); 207 differ, 158 only in texture ids, the rest a few other words in re-exported models; 5 terrain chunks, 3 of other sizes; 450 / 446 textures, 380 shared. `L01D2`: 253 / 253; 188 differ, 157 only in texture ids; 4 chunks, 3 of other sizes; 459 / 447, 391 shared. `L03A`: 282 / 281, **one texture animator (type 2) missing** (12 against 11); 193 differ; one chunk 188,068 → 187,588 bytes; 442 / 439, 431 shared. `L03ACOM`: 122 / 121, **one animator missing** (15 against 14); 90 differ; 47,408 → 47,376 bytes; 378 / 377, 369 shared. `L03A2`: 286 / 286; 170 differ; 203,116 → 198,196 bytes (3,439 → 3,332 records); 426 / 428, 423 shared. `L03B`: 114 / 114; 3 differ (Bugs's model and two templates, re-made bitmaps); the terrain identical; 382 / 382 all shared; the text block differs. `L03C`: 264 / 264 (the enemy 44 and its trigger 48 stand about 65 units away, 344); 132 differ; 2,206 words of the chunk, 2,115 of them texture ids; 447 / 449, 446 shared. `L04B3`: 108 / 108, **every object and resource byte-identical**; the one chunk 29,772 → 44,656 bytes (508 → 833 records), another mesh: **373 reads it (the same surface plus a black floor)**; 304 / 304 textures, identical. `LB04`: 124 / 124; 78 differ; 1,075 words of the chunk, all texture ids renumbered; 334 / 335, 333 shared. In all nine: **the collision blocks byte-identical, the zones, the header, the sounds and the music table identical; every object's script (states, steps, rules, flags, position, culling) the same** but for the objects named; what differs is the picture. **Corrects 344:** "the 8-bit files lack 12 and 14 texture animators" was a matching by resource counts, which the renumbering changes: one animator is missing in each; the rest of 344 stands. **The rule.** None about objects: **the `_8` files are the same levels exported for the 256-colour renderer**; for play, glitches and routing the two versions are the same level; for the 100% the one difference is the carrot 26 of `L01D1`, which is simply not in that export. | On the disc, with the bar set before the run: the nine LevIDs the swap names had to be exactly the nine `_8` files of the disc, none left over (**9 of 9**, 344); the deep comparison was made both ways (the normal file against the `_8` and back), every object matched by its whole script in three passes, every differing word of a model or sprite looked up in both texture tables (a renumbered id naming the same bitmap, a re-made bitmap, or other data), and the collision, zone, sound and music blocks compared byte by byte: identical in all nine. Not run, in the game: with `config.pc +4` = 0 (the 8-bit software renderer) enter `L01D1` from any way in (its rabbit hole, the level select, a warp by memory) and the carrot at (25910, −1800, 8190) must be missing every time, while the 24-bit software renderer and the 3D card show it; enter `L03C` the same way: the enemy 44 must stand about 65 units from its normal place. Wrong if a way in loads the normal file under the 8-bit renderer, if the carrot is missing with the 24-bit renderer, or if any other object is gone from a `_8` level than the three named. |
| 372 | **The sound glitch beyond the PC's 50: the PlayStation's code has no volume path at all** (the SPU master and CD volumes and the CD mute are written once at start-up, `CdMix`'s code is never called, the level files carry no menu actions), so its XA music cannot be muted the PC's way; on the PC the silence has one gate, Music Volume under 50, and three roads to it (an area change, a level load, the end of the running track), it survives a restart through `config.pc`, and the sound effects have no trap. `CODE` (PC: `music_start` `0x41D6B0`, `music_by_area` `0x41D980`, the update `0x41E330`, the stop `0x41D640`, action `0x51`, the F2 / F3 handlers `0x40E5C4` / `0x40E606`; PlayStation `SLUS_008.38`: the rule tables at `0x8005E3F0` (conditions) and `0x8005E560` (actions, 0x66 entries), the SPU setters `0x8004D840` / `0x8004D860` / `0x8004D880`, `CdMix` at `0x80047634` / `0x80047BAC`, the CD command callers). Completes 360; the PlayStation's options screen itself was not read (open). (N79.) **The PlayStation.** Its music is XA (`XA/MUSIC.XA`): its loudness is the SPU's CD-input volume and the CD-ROM's mixer. **Both are written once, at start-up** (`0x8003F984`: main volume `0x3FFF` / `0x3FFF`, the control bit, CD volume `0x3FFF` / `0x3FFF`); no other call reaches those setters nor the attribute setter's path; **`CdMix` is in the executable twice and called by nobody**; Mute / Demute (CD commands `0x0B` / `0x0C`) are sent only by the CD initialisation and by a walk over the commands 5…12; no compare with 50 near a loaded word. **The menus are code on the PlayStation, not level rules**: its action table stops at `0x65` (the PC's volume actions `0x7A`…`0x7F` do not exist), and its `MERLIN.BZZ` has 4 pause-bit objects and no action above `0x65`, against 8 objects and 27 uses in the PC's file. **So there is no threshold because there is no volume word: the PlayStation never changes the music's loudness after start-up and never mutes the CD; the PC's silence cannot happen there.** What its options screen offers (a bar with no effect, the effects only, or a Stereo / Mono choice) is not read. **The PC: the roads.** The one gate is `config.pc +0x34` under 50 (`bugs.exe+0x5CA854`; the compare at `0x41D6F1`, `0x41D990`, `0x41E346`). It is reached by **an area change** (no new track, the old plays on at its DirectSound volume), **a level load** (the reset stops the stream, nothing starts: silence from the first tick), **the end of the running track** (`0x41E580` restarts the last track when the stream has stopped, and the restart is refused under 50: silence without any change of area), and **F3 twice** (the restart is refused). **It survives a restart of the game**: the value is in `config.pc` (written by Apply and at the exit). Raising the value back to 50 unlocks it only at the next start trigger. Not roads: an area without an entry in the level's music table is silence by design (`0x41DA65` stops the stream) and unlocks itself at the next area with an entry; the lock byte `0x4B28C8` is dead (its three writers clear it); film mode gives the level's own music back when it ends. **The sound effects** (`+0x30`): a plain multiplier in the mixer, 0 is silence and any value acts at once; F2 puts the value aside and restores it (100 when the aside is 0): no trap. | The PlayStation's executable was searched whole for every path to the SPU volume registers and the CD mixer (the setters and their callers, the CD command senders, every compare of a loaded byte with 50): one caller each, at start-up, and none for `CdMix`; a volume path missed by that search would falsify the row. Not run: on the PC, with Music Volume under 50, stay in one area until the running track ends: the music must not restart (silence without any change of area); quit the game with the value under 50 and start it again: the next level load must be silent; raise it to 50 or more: the music must return only at the next area change or level load. On the PlayStation (BizHawk, RAM Watch): the SPU registers `0x1D80` / `0x1D82` (main) and `0x1DB0` / `0x1DB2` (CD) must read `0x3FFF` from the first level to the last whatever the options screen is set to. Wrong if the track restarts under 50, if the restart of the game forgets the value, if a level starts with music under 50, or if any setting of the PlayStation's screen changes those registers. |

The writer is the player handler, right after its box block-move (`object_box_block_move(Bugs, move, work, 0x9A690A)`, 300 / 322): the function walks **the ordering chain** (the list the culling pass builds every frame, 294: an object hidden by the distance, area or screen test and without the keep-running bit is not in it), takes the objects with `+8 & 0x9A690A`, class 2, not Bugs nor his parent or child, with a box, and tests the **16-bit** plan distance, **the Y overlap of the two boxes** and the rotated box; **an object with `+8 & 0x2000000` is a hit that corrects nothing** (the collectibles: `0x02001180`, `0x02001100`, `0x02000100`…). Back in the handler, **if the hit's `+8` has `0x100` or `0x20000`, the hit gets `+0x14 \|= 0x8000`**. The object's own rule walk then fires (366): the burst is cloned and the carrot dies; the burst counts when its animation ends (370) and **carries the keep-running bit** (`0x40` / `0x62` / `0x63`, 36 of the 37 burst templates), so the count survives Bugs's leaving (the exception: *Vort "X" Room*, `L05C`, word `0x02` with the template's own draw distance 7,000: its burst stops 7,000 from the camera); a golden carrot or a clock counts on the touch tick itself. **So the rule:** on a tick in which the handler reaches its block-move, **Bugs's box (±70 in x, ±40 in z, 400 tall above his origin, turned by his heading) overlaps the object's box grown by 4**, and the object is in the chain. For a plain carrot (box ±120 in the plan, 160 tall above its origin): Bugs's origin **in the plan within about 194 × 164 of the carrot's, and between 404 above his feet and 164 below them** (a standing jump adds 383). **Nothing else is asked: no zone, no trigger, no collision ground under Bugs, no move** (the test runs with the move he wants, zero included), **no area of his** (only the culling asks about areas: `0x22` / `0xA2` for most collectibles, the camera within 4,000…10,000 and the screen once culled; about 370 add the area test). **The second mechanism (37).** The carrots of *"Witch" way to Albuquerque? 1* and *2* (`L02B1` 88…99, 142, 163…168; *"Witch" way to Albuquerque? 2* (`L02B2`) 23…28, 67; flags 0), the golden carrots of *What's cookin', Doc? 3* (`L02A2` 138…142) and of the *Era selector* (`LS01` 24, 43, 71, 121, 160) and a few more have no touch rule: **their rule's effect `0x8000000` measures the distance from the object's origin to Bugs's origin (32-bit; in 3D with effect bit 1, else in the plan) against the rule's p24** — 200 in 3D for the carrots (his feet within 200: less than the box's reach), 300 in 3D, 500 in the plan… Apart from both: 16 type 16 spawners and 73 holders (crates, chests, enemies whose rule of another kind clones or counts one): not things to pick. **What is left to a teleport.** (1) The object in the chain (the camera within its distance; the area test where the word has bit 0). (2) The tick: the write puts Bugs at the new position, the block-move runs on it that tick, and the touch is marked before any ground snap (the snap is the next tick's, 355): **a Bugs written into a carrot's box takes it even under the floor**. (3) The box test's 16-bit plan distance repeats every 65,536 units (300); the rule-walk distance is 32-bit. (4) The golden carrots held in state 2 until a level byte reveals them (370) are not in their touch state until then. **The census, its numbers declared** (object by object; this row keeps the totals and the cases that matter). Every placed carrot, golden carrot, clock, rabbit hole and exit of the 52 playable files (the cutscenes, menus and `_8` twins out; the objects parked on the plan origin skipped): **756 carrots, 86 clocks, 232 golden carrots, 27 rabbit holes, 25 exits**. Each is judged by its reach band (the box grown by Bugs's half box and the margin, or the rule's radius) against the collision ground under its footprint and against a flood over the collision from the level's start, its restart points and its teleport targets (a rise of at most 489 per sub-cell, any drop, `0x7E` not walked). Flagged: **493**: 358 **unreached** (the flood alone: it knows no object boxes, lifts, poles, super-jump devices, vehicles or water, so it is a proxy and most of these are reached in play by one of those), 73 **high** (every ground under the footprint is farther below than a standing jump plus Bugs's box reaches), 57 **void** (over `0x7E`), 4 **under** (every ground under the footprint is above the object's box top), 1 **wall** (a `0x7F` sub-cell); 10 files have nothing flagged; the mine-cart rides *Mine or mine? 2* (`L03C1`) / *Mine or mine? 3* (`L03C2`) are not judged (no ground under the start). **The five the collision itself rules out:** the clock 159 of *Magic Hare Blower 1* (`L01D1`, (2705, −1760, 31180), box top 69 under the ground; a lift beside it), **the clock 157 of *Mine or mine? 1* (`L03C`, (8346, −1434, 16610), 935 under the ground −2460, nothing standable within 800)**, the exit 7 of *The Conquest for Planet X!* (`L05B1`, 28 under, changes level on a byte), the exit 72 of *La Corrida* (`LB04`, in a `0x7F` wall) and **rabbit hole 114 of the *Era selector* (`LS01`, (17910, 15300, 23200), 598 under the Dimension X island's ground: the warp of the pole glitch, N23)**. | N2 | **The pole glitch of the 1930s hub of the *Era selector* (`LS01`), as the code and the data predict it:** the sky changes only inside Dimension X's collision block, Y included, and the warp is either a radius gate that ignores Y or a 16-bit box test that repeats every 65,536 units. `HYPOTHESIS` on the PC: nothing measured there; on the PlayStation `GAME` for the skybox change inside the block and for the 16-bit warp, read on a recording (below). `CODE` for the area rule and for the two position tests, `DATA` for the blocks and the objects; `GAME` for the glitch itself, as players know it: from the lamp post near the rabbit hole of *The Greatest Escape*, Bugs goes down through the hub and falls for ever; hurrying towards Dimension X the skybox changes all at once; once it has changed, at a certain X/Z, while Y keeps growing, the game warps to the level of the entrance Bugs falls on (*Vort "X" Room* (`L05C`) in the recording below). **Checked on the PlayStation version** (a BizHawk recording with the coordinates on screen: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ytUZS2IEJww, 0:09 to 1:23; the positions are read off the screen; no count of time in seconds, since the PlayStation's ticks a second are not read, 316): at 0:29.5 the sky becomes Dimension X's with Bugs at (20391, 15667, 13996), a Y under the block's limit 16000 (the skybox change comes first, as read); at 0:56 Bugs is at the X and Z of entrance 114 (17893, 49183, 23127) and nothing happens; at 1:10.5 he is at (17893, 79563, 23127) and at 1:11.2 he is attached to entrance 114 (17910, 15300, 23200): the warp at a Y of about 81,000 = the entrance's Y + 65,536, that is the 16-bit test (the second outcome of run c below), not the radius gate that ignores Y. On the PC these remain the code's predictions, not run. **The data.** Collision blocks of `LS01`, one per area (two stacked for area 1), Y down, a block holds `top < y <= base`:<br>area 1, Stone Age: x 0…6400, z 0…6400, y −3200…0 (two slabs)<br>area 2, Medieval: x 6080…12160, z 0…11520, y −21000…−16000<br>area 3, Pirates: x 0…15680, z 14400…24000, y −32000…−16000<br>area 4, the 1930s: x 16000…24640, z 0…9600, y −3200…0<br>area 5, Dimension X: x 12160…23040, z 14080…31360, y **0…16000**<br>area 6, the arrival: x 26560…35520, z 0…10240, y −3200…1200.<br>Dimension X is under the level of the 1930s hub and 4480 units further in Z. No zone of `LS01` kills (no death floor), none loads a level; no cell of its heightmaps carries a flag nibble. **The skybox** (`CODE`: `0x431A60`, `0x436CD0`; the Y test is 292): the area in use is the area of the block containing the camera, else of the block containing Bugs, else it does not change; a block contains a point when X, Z and Y are inside, all compared in 32 bits. Below the 1930s hub (y > 0) Bugs and the camera are in no block: the sky stays the 1930s'. It becomes Dimension X's at the frame one of them enters x 12160…23040, z 14080…31360 with 0 < y <= 16000; after that, falling below 16000, no block again: the last area, 5, stays. **The warp: two mechanisms read.** What loads a level is effect bit `0x80000` of a rule (O101). In `LS01` only objects carry it, all of type 14; in Dimension X: object 120 at (13420, 15790, 23220) → LevID 49 *The Conquest for Planet X!* (`L05B1`); object 114 at (17910, 15300, 23200) → LevID 50 `L05C`; object 119 at (17900, 15330, 18130) → LevID 44 *Train your Brain! 1* (`L05A3A`); object 118 at (17890, 15330, 28320) → the film `CC5A`. (1) The radius gate of the type 14 handler (`0x443524`, effect bits `0x18000000`): `dx` and `dz` subtracted in 32 bits, `dy` only if effect bit 1 is set, squared, summed, square root, compared with the rule's radius. Almost every player gate of the rabbit holes of `LS01` has no bit 1 (radius 140 to 1000): it fires at any depth. Entrances 118 and 119 go to their loading state from a gate of radius 140 without Y, together with condition `0x31` (a button of the pad word `config+0x1000A`, 344). Checked on the PlayStation version: an action-key entrance of Dimension X fires during the fall of the pole glitch, far below the object. (2) The 16-bit tests: the box collision `0x431510` (`0x4313A0` in the other build; it is the camera's: Bugs's own point-in-box search is `0x4334F0`, 16-bit as well) adds the move to the position in 32 bits and then takes the low words (`sub ax, word [object+0xE4]`, the same for Y and Z); the contact test of the rule walker (`0x434B00`) does the same; the zone test (`0x434C70`) too (290). So every object box and every zone exists again every 65,536 units on each axis, Y included: the "parallel universes" of this engine. Entrances 114 and 120 start their loading sequence from rules masked on object flag `0x8000` of `object+0x14` ("Bugs touches it": its writers are in N6, what fires 114 in N23). The first copy of the Dimension X entrances below the real ones is at y ≈ 80,800 to 81,400; the fall is capped at 70 units a tick (`0x4370B1`), 2,100 a second, so that band, about 64,500 units below the Dimension X floor, comes after about 31 seconds of free fall. The word compared is the pad word at MainRAM `0x010010` `& 0xFFF6`: **L2 (`0x01`) and R1 (`0x08`) are masked out, not required**: the published "hold L2 + R1" is allowed, not needed; any other button or direction held breaks the match. All seven begin `0040 0080 0002 0004 0020 0040` = cross, square, R2, L1, circle, cross, as published; then three presses of `0x80` and `0x10`, counting in binary with `0x80` = 0 and `0x10` = 1: case 0 `80 80 80`, 1 `80 80 10`, 2 `80 10 80`, 3 `80 10 10`, 4 `10 80 80`, 5 `10 80 10`, 6 `10 10 80`. In the pad's layout (`0x10` triangle, `0x20` circle, `0x40` cross, `0x80` square: the opening itself uses `0x20` for the press every site calls "circle") **`0x10` is TRIANGLE, not circle**: `HYPOTHESIS` from the table: the endings would be square / triangle, so the codes as commonly printed with circle would not work; not tried in the game. `GAME`: the level select, the only one tried, is all squares and fits both readings: entered on the USA disc in BizHawk, the levels opened; and with every era open, clearing bit `0x01` of MainRAM `0x010064` by hand closed the rabbit holes of `LS01` again and setting it opened them at once, so the level select is that bit and can be set without the pad. **On the PC:** function `0x447CF0`, table at `0x4AE130` (seven entries of `0x18` bytes), called every tick, unconditionally, at the head of the live-list walk (`0x447EF5`): in any level, paused or not. It reads `config+0x10010`, a word in the PlayStation pad's raw layout (built at `0x449857`… in the input builder: low byte = NOT of the raw byte `0x4B31C3`, the buttons: `0x01` L2, `0x02` R2, `0x04` L1, `0x08` R1, `0x10` triangle, `0x20` circle, `0x40` cross, `0x80` square; high byte = NOT of `0x4B31C2`: directions, start, select). Gate: both `0x10` and `0x20` down, that is **the keys bound to triangle and circle held**; then the rest of the word, whenever it changes and is not 0, must equal the next press of a sequence: one button at a time, released before the next, no direction held; a wrong press resets that sequence. The PC sequences are eight presses of square `0x80`, R1 `0x08`, cross `0x40` and L1 `0x04`, not the PlayStation's nine; all begin square, R1, cross, L1; case · cheat · the last four presses · with the default keys (hold A + S):<br>0 · level select · square, R1, square, L1 · F R D E **F R F E**<br>1 · 99 carrots · square, R1, cross, L1 · F R D E **F R D E**<br>2 · all abilities · cross, R1, cross, L1 · F R D E **D R D E**<br>3 · full health · cross, R1, square, L1 · F R D E **D R F E**<br>4 · extra key · square, cross, R1, L1 · F R D E **F D R E**<br>5 · complete ending · cross, square, R1, L1 · F R D E **D F R E**<br>6 · incomplete ending · R1, cross, square, L1 · F R D E **R D F E**.<br>Which keys: the keyboard reader `0x402290` builds the pad from the DirectInput key table (`0x9CA8C0` + scan code). Directions, start and select are fixed: the four arrows, Enter (start, scan code `0x1C`), Tab (select, `0x0F`). The eight buttons are the eight scan codes at offsets `0x4A`…`0x51` of the settings file `config.pc` (128 bytes, next to `bugs.exe`; in memory from `0x9CA86A`), in the order **L2, R2, L1, R1, triangle, circle, cross, square**. The file shipped on the disc has `10 11 12 13 1E 1F 20 21` = **Q, W, E, R, A, S, D, F** by position on the keyboard; if the keys were changed in the game's options, those eight bytes of one's own file say which they are. A joystick goes through `0x4023E0` instead (`0x45F2B0` = 1), which reads the same eight bytes. There is **no other table of sequences**: the opening shared by the seven occurs eight times in the file: the seven entries, and once more inside entry 1 (whose second half repeats the opening); what follows the table (`0x4AE1D8`) is the button translation tables of the control schemes, and `config+0x10010` has no other reader that compares sequences. The PC sequences have not been tried in the game. **Seen in passing, of interest for TAS work:** the input builder (`0x449330`) holds a recorder and a player of pad input of its own: mode word `0x4B1D04` (1 = write the two raw pad bytes of every tick into a buffer at `[0x4B1CA4] + 0x100000`, 2 = read them back), position `0x4B1D08`, length `0x4B1D0C`. Who sets the mode was not read (N50, N36, N44). **Corrected on `$gp` by 351.** | (Triangle) enter cross, square, R2, L1, circle, cross and then triangle, square, square: save byte 8 (MainRAM `0x010048`) must gain `0x80`; the same ending in circle, square, square, as printed, must do nothing; L2 + R1 held or not must make no difference; wrong if the circle version works. (PC keys) with the disc's `config.pc`, hold A + S and press F, R, D, E, F, R, F, E one at a time, no arrow held: save byte 36 (`config+0x10064`) must gain bit 1 and the rabbit holes of `LS01` open, in any level and in the pause menu too; wrong if the byte does not move. |

| N68 | **Blend mode 2 (B − F) on the PC: at the upload the texel keeps its colour and gets alpha = its luminance (0.299 R + 0.587 G + 0.114 B of the 5-bit-to-8 texel),** then the gamma table on the colour only; at the draw it takes the one blend of the frame with the vertex colour as any other face: the silhouettes come out darker than the sky but not black; the PlayStation's true subtraction makes them nearly black. `CODE` (`texture_convert_for_mode` `0x420D90`: the mode dispatch `0x420E82`…`0x420E92`, table `0x4217A4`: mode 0 → `0x420E99`, 1 → `0x420F40`, **2 → `0x420FC8`**, 3 → `0x421019`, 4 (opaque) → `0x4210BF`; the constants at `0x45C9E8` = 0.299, `0x45C9F0` = −0.587, `0x45C9F8` = −0.114; the gamma and contrast tables `0x4210C8`…`0x42113C`; the blended-face queue `0x414620`, colours through the byte-to-float table `0x467B50`; the flush `0x412540`). The case: the flocks of *The Conquest for Planet X!* (`L05B1`), object 9, texture 7, light grey silhouettes 115…198 on a transparent ground, mode 2, vertex colours (133, 82, 73). **At the upload (`0x420FC8`):** `alpha = ftol(0.299 R − (−0.587 G) − (−0.114 B))` = **the luminance of the 8-bit texel** (the 5-bit palette channels shifted to 8, before the gamma), truncated; **the three colour bytes are not touched by the mode** (modes 0, 1 and 3 push them to full brightness by adding 255 − max; mode 2 does not). Then, as for every mode, when `0x4AC034` is set (every OpenGL profile, N9): each colour byte through the gamma table `0x4B5B40` (`(c/256)^(1/1.2)`, `0x421173`…) and the contrast table `0x4D5D40`; the alpha byte is not passed through either. Palette word 0 → alpha 0 and the cut-out mark (`0x420E62`). So 305's row for mode 2 is what the code does: **alpha = luminance, colour unchanged (then the gamma of every texture).** **At the draw:** a face whose blend byte says mode 2 (bits 5–6, N9) takes the texture copy made for mode 2 and face alpha 1.0; its vertex colours go into the queue as `byte / 255` (`0x4146B0`…`0x4146F6`), like any face; it is drawn in the sorted pass with **the one blend function of the frame, `GL_SRC_ALPHA, GL_ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA`** (`0x413011`: `[0x4AC028]`, `[0x4AC02C]`, the profile's; `GL_ONE, GL_SRC_COLOR` on the `Direct3D` profile only), alpha test off, depth read and written (346). No `glBlendEquation` is called anywhere (the program has `glBlendFunc` and `glAlphaFunc` only), and nothing zeroes or inverts a colour for mode 2, at the upload or at the draw. **The number, per pixel, for a grey texel T, a vertex colour V and a background B:** a = T / 255 (a grey texel: alpha = its value); C = 255 · (T / 255)^(1 / 1.2) · V / 255; out = a · C + (1 − a) · B. Texel T · alpha · T after gamma · C = T' × (133, 82, 73) / 255 · over a sky (110, 150, 240) · over a pale sky (190, 210, 240):<br>115 · 0.45 · 131 · (68, 42, 38) · (91, 101, 149) · (135, 134, 149)<br>150 · 0.59 · 164 · (86, 53, 47) · (96, 93, 126) · (129, 118, 126)<br>198 · 0.78 · 207 · (108, 67, 59) · (108, 86, 99) · (126, 99, 99).<br>**So on the PC the flocks are dark greyish-purple dashes, darker than the sky by a third to a half, never black** (the darkest a texel of 198 can give over that sky is (108, 86, 99)); orange dashes come out only if the vertex colour is applied without the alpha, or the alpha without the darkening of the sky. **On the PlayStation mode 2 is a true subtraction (the GPU's semi-transparency mode 2, a fact of the hardware, not of the game's code), `back − texel × vertex / 128`** (the PlayStation's neutral is 128, 268): the same texel of 150 over the same sky gives (0, 54, 154): a blue-black silhouette. The two machines differ here, as for the torch (N7); a renderer that imitates the PC must give the numbers above. | None on the disc: a reading of the code. In the game, not run: a lossless screenshot of the PC game on OpenGL in *The Conquest for Planet X!* with a flock over the sky: a pixel of a silhouette must be within a few units of the table above (no numeric bar set before) for the sky colour next to it (darker than the sky, greyish-purple, never orange and never black); on the PlayStation the same flock must be nearly black. Wrong if the PC's silhouettes are black, or orange, or lighter than the sky. |
| N69 | **Depth and order of the semi-transparent faces and the sprites on the PC: no `glDepthMask` in the program (depth is written by every pass), `GL_LEQUAL`;** opaque faces first, then the sorted list (cut-outs and blended faces, farthest first, alpha test OFF), then the sprites (unsorted, alpha test ON with `GL_GREATER 0`); a blended face or sprite cannot make a hole because nothing that should show through it is drawn after it, except the sprites among themselves. `CODE` (the flush `0x412540` whole, as a list of state changes; the start-up state `0x40DB58`…`0x40DC32`; every `glEnable` / `glDisable` / `glDepthFunc` / `glAlphaFunc` / `glBlendFunc` of the program; N38 and 346 had the pieces, this note puts the whole frame in one place with the state of each pass). The case: the torch's glow of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* (`L03A`), which in a renderer written outside the game made a hole in which the objects behind vanished inside the sprite's square; the game shows no hole (345's lossless screenshot of the torch: `GAME`). **The state at start-up (`0x40DB58`…):** `glDepthFunc(GL_LEQUAL)`, `glEnable(GL_DEPTH_TEST)`, `glBlendFunc(GL_SRC_ALPHA, GL_ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA)`, **`glAlphaFunc(GL_GREATER, 0)`**, dither, lighting, cull face, blend and alpha test off. **There is no `glDepthMask` in the program**: depth writes are on for every pass, and nothing switches the depth test off. One `glClear(colour \| depth)` a frame (`0x41256E`); the projection is `glOrtho(…, 65536)` with the vertex's own view depth as z (346): the depth buffer is linear in the view depth. **The passes of a frame, in order** (pass · where · texture · blend · alpha test · what is drawn):<br>1 · `0x41258A` · off · off · off · the untextured faces (triangles, quads), `glColor3f`<br>2 · `0x412D2D` · on · off · off · per texture, in texture order: **every opaque face** (textures with a transparent palette entry wait for pass 4)<br>3 · `0x413000` · off · **on**, `glBlendFunc([0x4AC028], [0x4AC02C])` = `GL_SRC_ALPHA, GL_ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA` on every profile but `Direct3D` · on · the untextured blended faces<br>4 · `0x41359A` · on · on · **off** (`0x4135B6`; on at `0x4135AE` only with `0x4AC030`, the `Direct3D` profile) · **the sorted list**: every cut-out and blended textured face of models and terrain, sorted farthest first by the mean view depth of its vertices (`0x41353E`), one by one<br>5 · `0x4137D3` · on · on · **on** (`GL_GREATER 0`) · **the sprites** (the `0x64` records: flame, glow, smoke, sparkles…), per texture bucket in list order, **not sorted**, all four corners at the object's view depth (N38)<br>then `0x413992`: `glBlendFunc(GL_SRC_ALPHA, GL_ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA)` back, `glFlush`, `glFinish`.<br>**What a transparent texel does in each pass.** Depth is written wherever a fragment passes the tests: in pass 4 (alpha test off) a texel with alpha 0 changes no colour **and writes its depth**; it cuts what is drawn later and farther: a sorted face out of order (the sort is by mean depth, per face), or a sprite of pass 5 standing behind a cut-out; everything opaque is already in the picture: no hole in the world (346). In pass 5 (alpha test `GL_GREATER 0`) **a texel with alpha exactly 0 is discarded, colour and depth**; a texel with alpha 1/255 or more is blended and writes its depth. The glow's texture (mode 1 copy: alpha = m − m/4, 0 only for a black texel) writes depth over its whole disc and nothing over its black corners. Since the sprites come last, **the only thing a sprite can cut is another sprite drawn after it in bucket order and standing behind it**: two glows in line, the farther drawn second, lose their overlap; a sprite behind a nearer opaque face is cut by the depth test, as it should be. **So the hole is an order, not a state.** In the game the glow's quad meets a finished picture: the depth test hides it where a wall or a crate is nearer, the blend shows the objects behind through its faint texels, and its black surround is discarded by the alpha test. A renderer gets the hole when it draws the sprite (or the blended face) with depth writes **before** something farther that should show through it: the sprite's depth then rejects the object. The game's recipe, exactly: (1) all opaque faces (world and followers together, the depth buffer shared); (2) the sorted list, farthest first, blending on, alpha test off (or discarding alpha 0 only: the same picture over a finished background), depth test and writes on; (3) the sprites, blending on, alpha test `GL_GREATER 0`, depth test and writes on, in the game's bucket order (texture id, then list order). A renderer that keeps extra layers of its own has two choices that keep the game's picture: draw them in pass 2 or 4 at the depth of the face they colour (they then behave as that face), or as a last overlay with the depth test on and **depth writes off**, a declared difference from the game, harmless because nothing of the game comes after them. What the code cannot say: the precision the driver gives the depth buffer (346), and what a `Direct3D`-profile driver does with the alpha test on in pass 4. | None on the disc: a reading of the code. In the game, not run: two torches of *Hey... What's up, Dock? 1* in line from the camera, the farther one later in the list: their glows must lose their overlap where the nearer glow's disc covers the farther one; a crate between the camera and a torch must cut the glow along the crate's edge; and the objects behind a glow must always show through its faint texels. Wrong if a glow ever hides an opaque object behind it, or if two glows in line never cut each other whatever their order. |
